The statement of the lemma follows from the commutativity of the two above diagrams and the universal property of the coproduct.

3.4.
Algebraic Theories

With a monad
M
one associates the Eilenberg-Moore category

\EM(M)
of
M
-algebras. Objects of

\EM(M)
are pairs
\mathbb{A}=(A,a)
of a set
A\in\mathbf{Sets}
and a map

a\colon MA\rightarrow A
,
making the first two
diagrams below commute.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{MA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{M^{2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{Ma}
\textstyle{MA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{MA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{Mh}
\textstyle{MB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{MA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{B}

A homomorphism from an algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,a)
to an algebra
\mathbb{B}=(B,b)
is a map
h\colon A\rightarrow B
between the underlying sets making the third diagram above commute. Hence the second diagram tells us that
a
is a homomorphism from
(MA,\mu)
to
(A,a)
. It is direct to check that
(MA,\mu)
is an Eilenberg-Moore algebra. Moreover,
(MA,\mu)
the free Eilenberg-Moore algebra generated by
A
.

In this paper we care for both categorical algebra, algebras of a monad, and their presentations in terms of algebraic theories and their models. An algebraic theory is a pair
(\Sigma,E)
of signature
\Sigma
(a set of operation symbols) and a set of equations
E
(a set of pairs of terms). A
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra, or a model of the algebraic theory
(\Sigma,E)
is an algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,\Sigma_{A})
with carrier set
A
and a set of operations
\Sigma_{A}
, one for each operation symbol in
\Sigma
, that satisfies the equations in
E
. A homomorphism from a
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,\Sigma_{A})
to a
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra
\mathbb{B}=(B,\Sigma_{B})
is a function
h\colon A\to B
that commutes with the operations, i.e.,
h\mathrel{\circ}f_{A}=f_{B}\mathrel{\circ}h^{n}
for all
n
-ary
f\in\Sigma
, and
f_{A},f_{B}
its interpretations in
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}
, respectively.

(\Sigma,E)
-algebras together with their homomorphisms form a category.

Every algebraic theory gives rise to a monad, defined by the free
\dashv
forgetful adjunction, see e.g.
[
Mac71
, V.6]
.
We now explicitly recall the construction.
In this construction, free algebras play an important role.
Recall that
\mathbb{F}_{X}=(FX,\Sigma_{FX})
is the free
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra generated by
X\subseteq FX
if for any
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,\Sigma_{A})
, any function
f\colon X\to A
extends to a unique homomorphism
f^{\#}\colon\mathbb{F}_{X}\to\mathbb{A}
. Free algebras are unique up to isomorphism.
For any set
X
, the free
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra generated by
X
is isomorphic to the algebra with carrier the set of
\Sigma
-terms with variable in
X
modulo
E
-equations. The adjunction give rise to the monad
T_{\Sigma,E}
of
\Sigma
-terms modulo
E
-equations. We next describe this monad.

Given a signature
\Sigma
, the monad
T_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
-terms maps a set
X
to the set of all
\Sigma
-terms with variables in
X
, and
f\colon X\to Y
to the function that maps a term over
X
to a term over
Y
obtained by substitution according to
f
. The unit maps a variable in
X
to itself, and the multiplication is term composition.
Given an algebraic theory
(\Sigma,E)
, the monad
T_{\Sigma,E}
of
\Sigma
-terms modulo
E
-equations is defined analogously, using equivalence classes of terms rather than plain terms.
The monad
T_{\Sigma,E}
is a quotient of
T_{\Sigma}
. Moreover, for two sets of equations
E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}
, the monad
T_{\Sigma,E_{2}}
is a quotient of
T_{\Sigma,E_{1}}
.

{defi}

A
presentation
of a monad
M
is an algebraic theory
(\Sigma,E)
such that the monad
T_{\Sigma,E}
is isomorphic to
M
.

Clearly,
(\Sigma,E)
is a presentation of the monad
T_{\Sigma,E}
.

Remark 4
.

A direct, syntactic, way to prove that
(\Sigma,E)
is a presentation of a monad
M
is to provide a monad isomorphism from
T_{\Sigma,E}
to
M
. We give such a direct syntactic proof for our monad of interest in
[
BSV21
]
. We now briefly discuss other ways to prove that
(\Sigma,E)
presents
M
, which justify our proof of the presentation in this paper (Theorem
12
).

Let
U\colon\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{Sets}
be the forgetful functor from the category
\mathbf{A}
of
(\Sigma,E)
-algebras to
\mathbf{Sets}

and let
F_{\Sigma,E}
be the left adjoint to
U
mapping a set X to the
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra of
\Sigma
-terms modulo
E
-equations. We have
T_{\Sigma,E}=UF_{\Sigma,E}
.
Now, suppose that
F
is another left adjoint to
U
. Since adjoints are unique up to isomorphism, there exists an isomorphism
\iota:F_{\Sigma,E}\Rightarrow F
. It is not difficult to derive from this that
U\iota:UF_{\Sigma,E}\Rightarrow UF
is an isomorphism from the monad
T_{\Sigma,E}=UF_{\Sigma,E}
to the monad
UF
.
Hence, in order to prove that
(\Sigma,E)
presents a monad
M
, it suffices to show that there exists some left adjoint
F
to the forgetful functor
U
such that
M=UF
. This is what we use to prove the presentation in this paper, in the proof of Proposition
8
below. The proposition itself identifies sufficient conditions for
M=UF
to hold, for an adjoint
F
to the forgetful functor
U
.

One can also work on the level of the categories of algebras.
The main observation is the following: a monad
M
is isomorphic to a monad
T
if and only if there is a
concrete
2
2

2

We thank Aloïs Rosset for reminding us of the necessity of a concrete isomorphism. Previous versions of this paper omitted mentioning that the isomorphism needs to be concrete, although our presentation proofs were not affected by this.
isomorphism
I
between their categories of Eilenberg-Moore algebras, as in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\EM(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\EM(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sets}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{T}}

In such a case, using uniqueness of adjoints up to isomorphism, we also have an isomorphism
\iota\colon F^{T}\Rightarrow IF^{M}
with
F^{M}
and
F^{T}
being the left adjoints of the forgetful functors
U^{M}
and
U^{T}
, respectively, and the monad isomorphism is given by

M=U^{M}F^{M}=U^{T}IF^{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle U^{T}\iota^{-1}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}U^{T}F^{T}=T.

In the other direction, given a monad isomorphism
\sigma\colon M\Rightarrow T
, we have that

\left(\!\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 11.15103pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.15103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-28.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.21724pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a^{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-49.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-28.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise-57.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)\mapsto\left(\!\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 12.93054pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\cr\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.93054pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{MX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-11.15103pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-46.91666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.21724pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a^{T}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-55.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise-62.55554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}\right)

is a concrete isomorphism of the algebras.
Hence, another way to prove that
(\Sigma,E)
is a presentation of
M
is to provide a concrete isomorphism between
\EM(T_{\Sigma,E})
and
\EM(M)
.

Moreover, as a consequence of Beck’s theorem (see
[
Mac71
, Theorem VI.8.1]
), there is a concrete isomorphism between
(\Sigma,E)
-algebras and
\EM(T_{\Sigma,E})
given by the comparison functor. Hence we get that
(\Sigma,E)
is a presentation of
M
if and only if there is a concrete isomorphism between the category of
(\Sigma,E)
-algebras and
\EM(M)
. This leads us to a third way to prove that
(\Sigma,E)
is a presentation of
M
: providing a concrete isomorphism between
(\Sigma,E)
-algebras and
\EM(M)
.
This way of providing a presentation was used in
[
MV20
,
MSV21
]
.

Such concrete isomorphism can be also obtained using again
[
Mac71
, Theorem VI.8.1]
. The theorem states that if
M
is the monad that arises from an adjunction

F\dashv U
for the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{Sets}
, then the comparison functor provides a concrete isomorphism from
(\Sigma,E)
-algebras to
\EM(M)
. Hence, our proof of the presentation using Proposition
8
can also be (indirectly) justified this way.

In the sequel, we present several algebraic theories that give presentations to the monads of interest.

Presenting the monad
\mathcal{P}_{ne}

Let
\Sigma_{N}
be the signature consisting of a binary operation
\oplus
.
Let
E_{N}
be the following set of axioms, stating that
\oplus
is associative, commutative, and idempotent, respectively.

\begin{array}[]{ccc}(x\oplus y)\oplus z&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(A)}}{{=}}&x\oplus(y\oplus z)\\
x\oplus y&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(C)}}{{=}}&y\oplus x\\
x\oplus x&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(I)}}{{=}}&x\end{array}

The algebraic theory
(\Sigma_{N},E_{N})
of
semilattices
provides a presentation for the monad
\mathcal{P}_{ne}
. We refer to this theory as the theory of nondeterminism. To avoid confusion later, it is convenient to fix here the interpretation of
\oplus
as a join (rather than a meet) and, thus, to think of the induced order as
x\sqsubseteq y
iff
x\oplus y=y
.

Presenting the monad
\mathcal{D}

Let
\Sigma_{P}
be the signature consisting of binary operations
+_{p}
for all
p\in(0,1)
.
Let
E_{P}
be the following set of axioms.

\begin{array}[]{ccc}(x+_{q}y)+_{p}z&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(A_{p})}}{{=}}&x+_{pq}(y+_{\frac{p(1-q)}{1-pq}}z)\\
x+_{p}y&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(C_{p})}}{{=}}&y+_{1-p}x\\
x+_{p}x&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(I_{p})}}{{=}}&x\end{array}

Here,
(A_{p})
,
(C_{p})
, and
(I_{p})
are the axioms of parametric associativity, commutativity, and idempotence.
The algebraic theory
(\Sigma_{P},E_{P})
of
convex algebras
, see
[
Ś74
,
Sem73
,
Dob06
,
Dob08
,
Jac10
]
, provides a presentation for the monad
\mathcal{D}
.

Another presentation of convex algebras is given by the algebraic theory with infinitely many operations denoting arbitrary (and not only binary) convex combinations
(\Sigma_{\hat{P}},E_{\hat{P}})
where
\Sigma_{\hat{P}}
consists of operations
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(\cdot)_{i}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}=1
and

E_{\hat{P}}
is the set of the following two axioms.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}x_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(P)}}{{=}}x_{j}

\displaystyle\text{ if }p_{j}=1

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{m}q_{i,j}x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(BC)}}{{=}}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}q_{i,j}\right)x_{j}.

Here,
(P)
stands for projection, and
(BC)
for barycentre.
This allows us to interchangeably use binary convex combinations or arbitrary convex combinations whenever more convenient. Moreover, we can write binary convex combinations
+_{p}
for
p\in[0,1]
and not just
p\in(0,1)
.

Convex algebras are known under many names: “convex modules” in
[
PR95
]
, “positive convex structures”
in
[
Dob06
]
(where
X
is taken to be endowed with the discrete topology), “sets with a convex structure”
in
[
Ś74
]
, and barycentric algebras
[
Sto49
]
.
We refer to the theory of convex algebras as the algebraic theory for probability.

Remark 5
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a
(\Sigma_{\hat{P}},E_{\hat{P}})
-algebra. Then (for
p_{n}\neq 1
and
\overline{p_{n}}=1-p_{n}
)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}x_{i}=\overline{p_{n}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{p_{j}}{\overline{p_{n}}}x_{j}\right)+p_{n}x_{n}.

(5)

Hence, an
n
-ary convex combination can be written as a binary convex combination using an
(n-1)
-ary convex
combination.

One can also see Equation (
5
) as a definition — the classical definition of Stone
[
Sto49
, Definition 1]
.
The following property, whose proof follows by induction along the lines of
[
Sto49
, Lemma 1–Lemma 4]
, gives the connection:

Let
X
be the carrier of a
(\Sigma_{P},E_{P})
-algebra.
Define
n
-ary convex operations inductively by the projection axiom and the formula (
5
). Then
X
becomes an algebra in
(\Sigma_{\hat{P}},E_{\hat{P}})
.

Presenting
\cdot+1

The algebraic theory
(\Sigma_{T},E_{T})
for the termination monad consists of a single constant (nullary operation symbol)
\Sigma_{T}=\{\star\}
and no equations
E_{T}=\emptyset
. This is called the theory of
pointed sets
.

Combining Algebraic Theories

Algebraic theories can be combined in a number of general ways: by taking their coproduct, their tensor, or by means of distributive laws (see e.g.
[
HPP02
]
). Unfortunately, these abstract constructions do not lead to a presentation for the monad we are interested in. We will thus devote the next section to show a “hand-made” presentation for this monad.

We conclude this section with a well known fact that can be easily proved, for instance using the distributive law in (
1
): given a presentation
(\Sigma,E)
for a monad
M
, the monad
M(\cdot+1)
is presented by the theory
(\Sigma^{\prime},E)
where
\Sigma^{\prime}
is
\Sigma
together with an extra constant
\star
.
For instance, the subdistributions monad
\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)
is presented by the theory
(\Sigma_{P}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{P})
of
pointed convex algebras
, also known as
positive convex algebras
. The theory
(\Sigma_{N}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{N})
of
pointed semilattices
provides instead a presentation for the monad
\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)
. It is interesting to observe that the powerset monad
\mathcal{P}
is presented by adding to
(\Sigma_{N}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{N})
the equation

\begin{array}[]{ccc}x\oplus\star&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B)}}{{=}}&x\end{array}

leading to the theory of
semilattices with bottom
. The theory of
semilattices with top
can be obtained by adding instead the following equation:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}x\oplus\star&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(T)}}{{=}}&\star.\end{array}

Similar axioms can be added to the theory of pointed convex algebras
(\Sigma_{P}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{P})
. The axiom

\begin{array}[]{ccc}x+_{p}\star&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B_{p})}}{{=}}&x\end{array}

makes the probabilistic structure collapse, i.e.,
x+_{p}y=x+_{q}y
holds for any
p,q\in(0,1)
:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}x+_{p}y&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B_{p})}}{{=}}&(x+_{q}\star)+_{p}y\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(A_{p})}}{{=}}&x+_{pq}(\star+_{\frac{p(1-q)}{1-pq}}y)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B_{p})}}{{=}}&x+_{pq}y\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B_{p})}}{{=}}&x+_{pq}(\star+_{\frac{q(1-p)}{1-pq}}y)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(A_{p})}}{{=}}&(x+_{p}\star)+_{q}y\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B_{p})}}{{=}}&x+_{q}y\end{array}

At the monad level, adding the axioms
(B_{p})
can be seen as the quotient of monads
\supp\colon\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}
mapping each sub-distribution into its support (e.g.,
(x+_{p}y)+_{q}\star
becomes
x+y
).

On the other hand, the axiom

\begin{array}[]{ccc}x+_{p}\star&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(T_{p})}}{{=}}&\star\end{array}

quotients the monad
\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)
into
\mathcal{D}+1
, recall Example
3.3
. Intuitively, each term of this theory is either a sum of only variables (a distribution) or an extra element (
\star
). This axiom describes the unique functorial way of adding termination to a convex algebra, the so-called black-hole behaviour of
\star
, cf.
[
SW18
]
.

4.
Algebraic Theory for Nondeterminism and Probability

In this section we recall the definition of the monad
C
for probability and nondeterminism, give its presentation via
convex semilattices
, and present examples of
C
-algebras.

4.1.
The monad
C
of convex subsets of distributions

The monad
C
origins in the field of domain theory
[
Mis00
,
J.G08
,
TKP09
]
, and in the work of Varacca and Winskel
[
Var03
,
VW06
]
. Jacobs
[
Jac08
]
gives a detailed study of (a generalisation of) this monad.

For a set
X
,
CX
is the set of non-empty, finitely-generated convex subsets of distributions on
X
, i.e.,

CX=\{S\subseteq\mathcal{D}X\mid S\neq\emptyset,\convex(S)=S,S\text{ is finitely generated}\}.

Recall that, for a subset
S
of a convex algebra,
\convex(S)
is the convex closure of
S
, i.e., the smallest convex set that contains
S
, i.e.,

\convex(S)=\{\sum p_{i}x_{i}\mid p_{i}\in[0,1],\sum p_{i}=1,x_{i}\in S\}.

We say that a convex set
S
is generated by its subset
B
if
S=\convex(B)
. In such a case we also say that
B
is a basis for
S
.
A convex set
S
is finitely generated if it has a finite basis.

For a function
f\colon X\to Y
,
Cf\colon CX\to CY
is given by

Cf(S)=\{\mathcal{D}f(d)\mid d\in S\}=\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S).

The unit of
C
is
\eta\colon X\to CX
given by
\eta(x)=\{\delta_{x}\}
.

The multiplication of
C
,
\mu\colon CCX\to CX
can be expressed in concrete terms as follows
[
Jac08
]
. Given
S\in CCX
,

\mu(S)=\bigcup_{\Phi\in S}\{\sum_{U\in\supp(\Phi)}\Phi(U)\cdot d\mid d\in U\}.

Hence,
d\in\mu(S)
if and only if there exists
\Phi\in S
and for all
U\in\supp(\Phi)
there exists
d_{U}\in U
such that
d=\sum_{U\in\supp(\Phi)}\Phi(U)\cdot d_{U}
.

For later use it is convenient to observe that the assignment
S\mapsto\convex(S)
gives rise to a natural transformation
[
Mio14
,
BSS17
]
, that we refer hereafter as

\convex\colon\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}\Rightarrow C

(6)

The following lemma summarises the relationship between the monads
\mathcal{P}_{ne}
,
\mathcal{D}
, and
C
. Note here that
\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}
is not a monad.

Lemma 6
.

Consider the natural transformations depicted in the diagram on the left below.

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}_{\mathcal{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\convex}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{ne}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{ne}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}}

Then

(1)

\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}=\convex\mathrel{\circ}\mathcal{P}_{ne}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}
is an injective monad map.

(2)

\chi^{\mathcal{D}}=\convex\mathrel{\circ}\eta^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}_{\mathcal{D}}
is an injective monad map.

Proof 4.1
.

Here, it is most important to notice that
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}(\varphi)=\{\varphi\}
, for any distribution
\varphi
, as singleton sets are convex, and
\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}(S)=\mathcal{D}S
. Keeping this in mind, it is not difficult to check that the diagrams needed for
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}
and
\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}
to be monad maps indeed commute. For the unit diagrams, we have:

\chi^{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\circ}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}(x)=\chi^{\mathcal{D}}(\delta_{x})=\{\delta_{x}\}=\eta^{C}(x)

and

\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}\mathrel{\circ}\eta^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}(x)=\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}(\{x\})=\{\delta_{x}\}=\eta^{C}(x).

For the multiplication diagrams, we derive, given
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}X
,

\chi^{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\circ}\mu^{\mathcal{D}}(\Phi)=\chi^{\mathcal{D}}\left(\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}X}\Phi(\varphi)\cdot\varphi\right)=\left\{\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}X}\Phi(\varphi)\cdot\varphi\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mu^{C}\mathrel{\circ}C\chi^{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\circ}\chi^{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{D}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{C}\mathrel{\circ}C\chi^{\mathcal{D}}(\{\Phi\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{C}\left(\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}X}\Phi(\varphi)\cdot\{\varphi\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}X}\Phi(\varphi)\cdot\varphi\right\}.

Given
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{i}\mid i\in I\}
for
S_{i}\subseteq X,S_{i}\neq\emptyset,I\neq\emptyset
, that is
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{P}_{ne}(X)
, we have

\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}\mathrel{\circ}\mu^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}({\mathcal{S}})=\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\right)=\mathcal{D}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\right)

and

\displaystyle\mu^{C}\mathrel{\circ}\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}C\mathrel{\circ}\mathcal{P}_{ne}\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}({\mathcal{S}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{C}\mathrel{\circ}\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}C(\{\mathcal{D}S_{i}\mid i\in I\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{C}\left(\mathcal{D}\{\mathcal{D}S_{i}\mid i\in I\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\right)

where the inclusion
\subseteq
in the last equality is obvious, and the inclusion
\supseteq
can be derived by simple grouping and normalisation, as follows. Given
d\in\mathcal{D}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\right)
, i.e.,

d=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}x_{j}
where
x_{j}\in\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}
, let
(J_{i}|i\in I)
be a partition of
J
with the property

j\in J_{i}\Rightarrow x_{j}\in S_{i}
, and let
p_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{j}
. Let, moreover,

d_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}\frac{p_{j}}{p_{i}}\cdot x_{j}

if
p_{i}\neq 0
, and
d_{i}=\delta_{x}
for some
x\in S_{i}
otherwise.
Then
d=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}d_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\{\mathcal{D}S_{i}\mid i\in I\}
.

Injectivity follows directly from
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}(\varphi)=\{\varphi\}
and
\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}(S)=\mathcal{D}S
, the latter since
\mathcal{D}S=\mathcal{D}S^{\prime}
if and only if
S=S^{\prime}
.

4.2.
The presentation of
C

We now introduce the algebraic theory
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
of
convex semilattices
, that gives us the presentation of
C
and thus provides an algebraic theory for nondeterminism and probability.

A convex semilattice
\mathbb{A}
is an algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,\oplus,+_{p})
with a binary operation
\oplus
and for each
p\in(0,1)
a binary operation
+_{p}
satisfying the axioms
(A),(C),(I)
of a semilattice, the axioms
(A_{p}),(C_{p}),(I_{p})
for a convex algebra, and the following distributivity axiom:

(x\oplus y)+_{p}z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(D)}}{{=}}(x+_{p}z)\oplus(y+_{p}z)

Hence,
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
is given by
\Sigma_{NP}=\Sigma_{N}\cup\Sigma_{P}
and
E_{NP}=E_{N}\cup E_{P}\cup\{(D)\}
.

In every convex semilattice there also holds a convexity law, of which we directly present the generalised version in the following lemma.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\mathbb{A}=(A,\oplus,+_{p})
be a convex semilattice. Then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, all
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
and all
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in[0,1]
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}=1
we have

a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}\oplus\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}a_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(C)}}{{=}}a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}.\qed

Proof 4.2
.

For
n=1
the property amounts to idempotence. Assume
n>1
and the property holds for
n-1
.

Below, we will write
(D)
also for generalised distributivity as in

\bigoplus_{i}a_{i}+_{p}\bigoplus_{j}b_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(D)}}{{=}}\bigoplus_{i,j}(a_{i}+_{p}b_{j}).

First, we observe that

a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}=a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}(a_{i}+_{p_{1}}\bigoplus_{j\neq i}a_{j})

(7)

which follows from

\displaystyle a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(I_{p})}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n})+_{p_{1}}(a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(D)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j}(a_{i}+_{p_{1}}a_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(I_{p},D)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}(a_{i}+_{p_{1}}\bigoplus_{j\neq i}a_{j})

Recall that we write
\overline{p}
for
1-p
if
p\in[0,1]
.
Furthermore, having in mind that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}a_{i}=a_{1}+_{p_{1}}(\sum_{i=2}^{n}\frac{p_{i}}{\overline{p_{1}}}a_{i})
we have

\displaystyle a_{1}+_{p_{1}}(\bigoplus_{j\neq 1}a_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle IH}}{{=}}

\displaystyle a_{1}+_{p_{1}}(\bigoplus_{j\neq 1}a_{j}\oplus\sum_{j\neq 1}\frac{p_{j}}{\overline{p_{1}}}a_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(D)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(a_{1}+_{p_{1}}\bigoplus_{j\neq 1}a_{j})\oplus(a_{1}+_{p_{1}}\sum_{j\neq 1}\frac{p_{j}}{\overline{p_{1}}}a_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a_{1}+_{p_{1}}\bigoplus_{j\neq 1}a_{j})\oplus\sum_{i}p_{i}a_{i}.

Using this in the second equality below, we get

\displaystyle a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}\oplus\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}a_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:gen-conv-aux})}}{{=}}a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}(a_{i}+_{p_{1}}\bigoplus_{j\neq i}a_{j})\oplus\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}a_{i}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}(a_{i}+_{p_{1}}\bigoplus_{j\neq i}a_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:gen-conv-aux})}}{{=}}a_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus a_{n}.

We next formulate a property that provides a way to prove that an algebraic theory is a presentation for a monad, which we will later apply in the proof that
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
is a presentation for
C
.

Proposition 8
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be the category of
(\Sigma,E)
-algebras with signature
\Sigma
and equations
E
. Let
U\colon\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{Sets}
be the forgetful functor. In order to prove that
(\Sigma,E)
is a presentation for a monad
(M,\eta,\mu)
, it suffices to:

1.

For any set
X
, define
\Sigma
-operations
\Sigma_{X}
on
MX
and prove that with these operations
(MX,\Sigma_{X})
is an algebra in
\mathbf{A}
. Moreover prove that for any map
f\colon X\to Y
,
Mf
is an
\mathbf{A}
-homomorphism from
(MX,\Sigma_{X})
to
(MY,\Sigma_{Y})
.

2.

Prove that
(MX,\Sigma_{X})
is the free algebra in
\mathbf{A}
generated by
X
, i.e., for any algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,\Sigma_{A})
in
\mathbf{A}
and any map
f\colon X\to A
, there is a unique homomorphism
f^{\#}\colon(MX,\Sigma_{X})\to\mathbb{A}
that extends
f
, i.e., that satisfies
f=Uf^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta
.

3.

Prove that
\mu_{X}=(\text{\emph{id}}_{MX})^{\#}
.

Proof 4.3
.

Assume that 1.-3. hold. We show that
M
is presented by
(\Sigma,E)
, as explained in Remark
4
.
We provide a left adjoint to the forgetful functor such that
M
is the monad that arises from this adjunction. We start with defining the adjunction explicitly.

Let
F\colon\mathbf{Sets}\to\mathbf{A}
be the functor defined on objects as
FX=(MX,\Sigma_{X})
. Hence,
UFX=MX
. On arrows
f\colon X\to Y
, we set
Ff=(\eta\mathrel{\circ}f)^{\#}
.

Then
F
is a left adjoint of the forgetful functor
U
, and the adjunction is given by the bijective correspondence
(f\colon X\to U\mathbb{A})\mapsto(f^{\#}\colon FX\to\mathbb{A})
. This is injective since:
f^{\#}=g^{\#}\Rightarrow f=Uf^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=Ug^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=g
. It is surjective since: for a homomorphism
h^{*}\colon FX\to\mathbb{A}
, we consider
h=Uh^{*}\mathrel{\circ}\eta
and since
h^{\#}
is the unique homomorphism such that
h=Uh^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta
, we get that
h^{\#}=h^{*}
.

Next, we see that
UFf=Mf
as a consequence of naturality of
\eta
. Namely, we have
that
Ff=(\eta\mathrel{\circ}f)^{\#}
is the unique homomorphism with the property
UFf\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\eta\mathrel{\circ}f
. Hence, using 1., since
Mf\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\eta\mathrel{\circ}f
by naturality of
\eta
, we get
UFf=Mf
.

Let
(T,\bar{\eta},\bar{\mu})
be the monad of this adjunction.
This means that, see e.g.
[
Mac71
, VI.1, IV.1]
,
T=UF
,
(\bar{\eta})^{\#}=\text{\emph{id}}_{FX}
and
\bar{\mu}=U\varepsilon F
where

\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}=(\text{\emph{id}}_{U\mathbb{A}})^{\#}
is the counit of the adjunction, and hence
\bar{\mu}_{X}=(\text{\emph{id}}_{MX})^{\#}
.
We next show that

\eta^{\#}=\text{\emph{id}}_{FX}
which implies
\bar{\eta}=\eta
. All we need to observe is that
U\text{\emph{id}}_{FX}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\text{\emph{id}}_{UFX}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\eta
and since
\eta^{\#}
is the unique homomorphism with
U\eta^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\eta
and
\text{\emph{id}}_{FX}
is a homomorphism from
FX
to itself, we get
\eta^{\#}=\text{\emph{id}}_{FX}
. Finally, item 3. proves that
\bar{\mu}=\mu
.

For
p\in[0,1]
we set
\overline{p}=1-p
. Let
X
be an arbitrary set. We define
\Sigma_{NP}
-operations on
CX
by

S_{1}\oplus S_{2}=\convex(S_{1}\cup S_{2})

and for
p\in(0,1)

S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}=\{\varphi\mid\varphi=p\varphi_{1}+\overline{p}\varphi_{2}\text{ for some }\varphi_{1}\in S_{1},\varphi_{2}\in S_{2}\}

where
p\varphi_{1}+\overline{p}\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{1}+_{p}\varphi_{2}
is the binary convex combination of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}X
, defined point-wise. Note that
S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}
is the Minkowski sum of two convex sets. If convenient, we may sometimes also write, as usual,
pS_{1}+\overline{p}S_{2}
for the Minkowski sum
S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}
.

The proof of the presentation follows the structure of Proposition
8
via the following three lemmas.

Lemma 9
.

With the above defined operations

(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
is a convex semilattice, for any set
X
. Moreover, for a map
f\colon X\to Y
, the map
Cf\colon CX\to CY
is a convex semilattice homomorphism from
(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
to
(CY,\oplus,+_{p})
. ∎

Lemma 10
.

The convex semilattice

(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
is the free convex semilattice generated by
\eta(X)
. ∎

Lemma 11
.

The multiplication
\mu
of the monad
C
satisfies
\mu=(\text{\emph{id}}_{CX})^{\#}
. ∎

All detailed proofs are in Section
4.3
, where we also list some additional helpful properties.
Now all ingredients are in place and we get the presentation for
C
directly from Proposition
8
.

Theorem 12
.

The theory for nondeterminism and probability
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
, i.e., the theory of convex semilattices, is a presentation for the monad
C
. ∎

Remark 13
.

Theorem
12
is to some extent known
3
3

3

Personal communication with Gordon Plotkin.
but we could not find a proof of it in the literature. In
[
Var03
,
VW06
]
a monad for probability and nondeterminism is given starting from a similar algebraic theory (with somewhat different basic algebraic structure).
The observation that the distributive law
(D)
gives rise to convex subsets was known at least since
[
MOW03
]
. Indeed, all the axioms in
E_{NP}
already appear in
[
MOW03
]
that additionally contains the axiom
(B)
and
(T_{p})
for dealing with termination. Nevertheless, in
[
MOW03
]
these axioms are not related to any particular monad.
There is also another possible way of combining probability with nondeterminism, by distributing
\oplus
over
+_{p}
(see e.g.
[
KP17
,
DPS18
]
).

Remark 14
.

Having the presentation enables us to identify and interchangeably use convex subsets of distributions and terms in
\Sigma_{NP}
modulo equations in
E_{NP}
. This is particularly useful in examples and our further developments. Note that in the syntactic view
\eta(x)
is identified with the term
x
.

The presentation is a valuable tool in many situations where reasoning with algebraic theories is more convenient than reasoning with monads. For instance, it is much easier to check whether a certain algebra is a
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
-model, than to check that it is an algebra for the monad
C
. We illustrate this with three
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
models that play a key role in our further results and exposition.

The max convex semilattice

\mathbb{M}\rm{ax}=([0,1],\max,+_{p})
is a
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
-algebra when taking
\oplus
to be
\max\colon[0,1]\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
and
+_{p}
the standard convex combination

+_{p}\colon[0,1]\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
with
x+_{p}y=p\cdot x+\overline{p}\cdot y
for
x,y\in[0,1]
. To check that this is a
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
model, it is enough to prove that
\max
satisfies the axioms in
E_{N}
, that
+_{p}
satisfies the axioms in
E_{P}
, and that they satisfy the axiom
(D)
, namely that
\max(x,y)+_{p}z=\max(x+_{p}z,y+_{p}z)
.

The min convex semilattice

\mathbb{M}\rm{in}=([0,1],\min,+_{p})

is obtained similarly by taking
\oplus
to be
\min\colon[0,1]\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
rather than
\max
, and gives another example of a
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
-algebra. It is indeed very simple to check that
([0,1],\min)
forms a semilattice and that the distributivity law holds.

The min-max interval convex semilattice

We consider the algebraic structure
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{I},\minmax,+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}})
for
\mathcal{I}
the set of intervals on
[0,1]
, i.e.,

\mathcal{I}=\{[x,y]\,|\,x,y\in[0,1]\text{ and }x\leq y\}.

For
[x_{1},y_{1}],[x_{2},y_{2}]\in\mathcal{I}
, we define
\minmax\colon\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{I}
as

\minmax([x_{1},y_{1}],[x_{2},y_{2}])=[\min(x_{1},x_{2}),\max(y_{1},y_{2})]

and
+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}}\colon\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{I}
by

[x_{1},y_{1}]+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}}[x_{2},y_{2}]=[x_{1}+_{p}x_{2},\;y_{1}+_{p}y_{2}]\text{.}

The fact that this is a model for
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
follows easily from the fact that
\mathbb{M}\rm{ax}
and
\mathbb{M}\rm{in}
are models for
(\Sigma_{NP},E_{NP})
.

Remark 15
.

The fact that
\maxalg
and
\minalg
are
C
-algebras on
[0,1]
was already proven in
[
vHHOS18
]
, without an algebraic presentation. Having the algebraic presentation significantly simplifies the proofs.

4.3.
Auxiliary Lemmas for the Proof of the Presentation of
C

Before we proceed with the proof of the presentation, we recall several properties that are known or immediate to check, but very helpful in our further proofs.

Lemma 16
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be two convex algebras, and
f\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
a convex homomorphism. Then for all
X\in\mathcal{P}_{u}A
, for
\mathcal{P}_{u}
being the unrestricted (not necessarily finite) powerset,
\convex_{\mathbb{B}}\overline{f}(X)=\overline{f}(\convex_{\mathbb{A}}X)
.
In particular, if
X
is convex then also
\overline{f}(X)
is convex.

Proof 4.4
.

For
\subseteq
, for an arbitrary
pf(x)+\overline{p}f(y)\in\convex_{\mathbb{B}}\overline{f}(X)
with
x,y\in X
, we have

pf(x)+\overline{p}f(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}f(px+\overline{p}y)\in\overline{f}(\convex_{\mathbb{A}}X)

where the equality marked by
(*)
holds by the assumption that
f
is a convex homomorphism.
For
\supseteq
, consider
f(a)\in\overline{f}(\convex_{\mathbb{A}}X)
. Then
a=px+\overline{p}y
for some
x,y\in X
. Since
f
is convex,
f(a)=pf(x)+\overline{p}f(y)
and
f(x),f(y)\in\overline{f}(X)
. Hence
f(a)\in\convex_{\mathbb{B}}\overline{f}(X)
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be two convex algebras, and
f\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
a convex homomorphism. Then the image map
\overline{f}=\mathcal{P}_{u}f\colon\mathcal{P}_{u}A\to\mathcal{P}_{u}B
, for
\mathcal{P}_{u}
being the unrestricted (not necessarily finite) powerset, is a convex map, i.e. if
S=X+_{p}Y
for
X\in\mathcal{P}_{u}A,Y\in\mathcal{P}_{u}B
, then
\overline{f}(S)=\overline{f}(X)+_{p}\overline{f}(Y)
.

Proof 4.5
.

Let
S=X+_{p}Y
for
X\in\mathcal{P}_{u}A,Y\in\mathcal{P}_{u}B
. Then

\displaystyle\overline{f}(S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{f(s)\mid s\in S\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{f(px+\overline{p}y)\mid x\in X,y\in Y\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\{pf(x)+\overline{p}f(y)\mid x\in X,y\in Y\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\overline{f}(X)+\overline{p}\overline{f}(Y).

and here, again,
(*)
holds since
f
is convex.

Lemma 18
.

Let
X
be a set and let
S\in\mathcal{P}_{u}CX
be a convex set with respect to Minkowski sum. Then
\bigcup S\in CX
.

Proof 4.6
.

Let
S=\{S_{i}\mid i\in I\}
. Let
\Phi,\Psi\in\bigcup S
. Then there exist
i,j\in I
with
\Phi\in S_{i}
and
\Psi\in S_{j}
. We have
p\Phi+\overline{p}\Psi\in pS_{i}+\overline{p}S_{j}\in S
as
S
is convex.

We can now prove Lemmas
9
,
10
, and
11
announced in the previous section, from which the presentation of
C
follows.

Proof 4.7

(Proof of Lemma
9
)
.

We want to prove the following:

(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
is a convex semilattice, for any set
X
. Moreover, for a map
f\colon X\to Y
, the map
Cf\colon CX\to CY
is a convex semilattice homomorphism from
(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
to
(CY,\oplus,+_{p})
.

In any convex algebra
\mathbb{A}
for
S,T\subseteq A
we have

\convex(\convex(S)\cup T)=\convex(S\cup T).

As a consequence, using the associativity of union, we get that the axiom
(A)
holds. For
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}\in CX
:

\displaystyle S_{1}\oplus(S_{2}\oplus S_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\convex(S_{1}\cup\convex(S_{2}\cup S_{3}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\convex(S_{1}\cup(S_{2}\cup S_{3}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\convex((S_{1}\cup S_{2})\cup S_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\convex(\convex(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\cup S_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S_{1}\oplus S_{2})\oplus S_{3}.

Commutativity and idempotence hold due to commutativity and idempotence of union.

Defining convex operations on
CX
using Minkowski sum, see
[
BSS17
]
, leads to a convex algebra, i.e.,
(A_{p}),(C_{p}),(I_{p})
hold.

The axiom
(D)
holds as:

\displaystyle(S_{1}\oplus S_{2})+_{p}S_{3}

\displaystyle=p\convex(S_{1}\cup S_{2})+\overline{p}S_{3}

\displaystyle=\{pqd_{1}+p\overline{q}d_{2}+\overline{p}d_{3}\mid q\in[0,1],d_{i}\in S_{i}\}

\displaystyle=\convex((pS_{1}+\overline{p}S_{3})\cup(pS_{2}+\overline{p}S_{3})).

Finally,
Cf
is a homomorphism from
(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
to
(CY,\oplus,+_{p})
as

\displaystyle Cf(S_{1}\oplus S_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{1}\oplus S_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\convex(\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{1}\cup S_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\convex(\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{1})\cup\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{1})\oplus\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Cf(S_{1})\oplus Cf(S_{2})

where the equality marked by
(a)
holds by Lemma
16
. Similarly

\displaystyle Cf(S_{1}+_{p}S_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{1}+_{p}S_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{1})+_{p}\overline{\mathcal{D}f}(S_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Cf(S_{1})+_{p}Cf(S_{2}).

where the equality marked by
(b)
holds by Lemma
17
.

Proof 4.8

(Proof of Lemma
10
)
.

We want to prove the following:
The convex semilattice

(CX,\oplus,+_{p})
is the free convex semilattice generated by
\eta(X)
.

We need to show that for any map
f\colon X\to A
for a convex semilattice
\mathbb{A}=(A,\oplus,+_{p})
, there is a unique convex semilattice homomorphism
f^{\#}\colon(CX,\oplus,+_{p})\to\mathbb{A}
such that
Uf^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=f
. So, let
\mathbb{A}=(A,\oplus,+_{p})
be a convex semilattice, and let
f\colon X\to A
be a map. We use the same notation for the operations in
A
and in
CX
for simplicity.

Note that, since any convex semilattice is a convex algebra, there is a unique convex homomorphism
f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}\colon\mathcal{D}X\to(A,+_{p})
, as
\mathcal{D}X
is the free convex algebra generated by
\eta_{\mathcal{D}}(X)
. Hence,
Uf_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}\mathrel{\circ}\eta_{\mathcal{D}}=f
.

Now, given a convex set
S=\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}\in CX
we put

f^{\#}(S)=f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{2})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n}).

We first prove that
f^{\#}
is well defined, which is the most important step. We show that whenever

\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}=\convex\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\}

(8)

then

\f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})=f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{m}).

Clearly, if Equation (
8
) holds, then for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
d_{i}\in\convex\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\}
and for all
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
e_{j}\in\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}
.
Hence,

\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n},e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\}=\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}=\convex\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}.

If we can prove that whenever
e\in\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}
then

f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e)=f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n}),

we would be done with well defined-ness as then

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})

\displaystyle=\quad f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{m})

\displaystyle=\quad f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{m}).

So, let
e\in\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}
. Then
e=\sum_{i}p_{i}d_{i}
and since
f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}
is a convex algebra homomorphism,
f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e)=\sum_{i}p_{i}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{i})
.
Now, by the convexity law, Lemma
7
, we have

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})\oplus\sum_{i}p_{i}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n}).

It remains to show that
f^{\#}
is a homomorphism and that it is uniquely extending
f
on
\eta(X)
. Let
S,T\in CX
. Let
S=\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}
,
T=\convex\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\}
.

Then
S\oplus T=\convex(S\cup T)=\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n},e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\}
and we get

f^{\#}(S\oplus T)=f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n})\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{m})=f^{\#}(S)\oplus f^{\#}(T).

Next, we first notice that
S+_{p}T=\convex\{pd_{i}+\overline{p}e_{j}\mid i\in\{1,\dots,n\},j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\}
.
For
\supseteq
, we see that

\sum_{i,j}q_{i,j}(pd_{i}+\overline{p}e_{j})=p\sum_{i,j}q_{i,j}d_{i}+\overline{p}\sum_{i,j}q_{i,j}e_{j}\in S+_{p}T.

For
\subseteq
, take
pd+\overline{p}e\in S+_{p}T
. So,
d=\sum_{i}q_{i}d_{i}
and
e=\sum_{j}r_{j}e_{j}
and we have

\displaystyle pd+\overline{p}e

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{i}q_{i}d_{i}+\overline{p}\sum_{j}r_{j}e_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{i}q_{i}\left(\sum_{j}r_{j}\right)d_{i}+\overline{p}\sum_{j}r_{j}\left(\sum_{i}q_{i}\right)e_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}q_{i}r_{j}(pd_{i}+\overline{p}e_{j}).

Now

\displaystyle f^{\#}(S+_{p}T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(pd_{i}+\overline{p}e_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j}pf_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{i})+\overline{p}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{i})+_{p}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(D)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(d_{n}))+_{p}(f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(e_{m}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\#}(S)+_{p}f^{\#}(T).

Finally, assume
f^{*}\colon(CX,\oplus,+_{p})\to\mathbb{A}
is another homomorphism that extends
f
on
\eta(X)
, i.e., such that
Uf^{*}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=f
.
Then
f^{*}(\{\delta_{x}\})=f^{\#}(\{\delta_{x}\})=f(x)
.
Since both
f^{\#}
and
f^{*}
are convex homomorphisms, and
\{\sum_{i}p_{i}x_{i}\}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\{\delta_{x_{i}}\}
, we get

f^{*}(\{\sum_{i}p_{i}x_{i}\})=\sum_{i}p_{i}f^{*}(\{\delta_{x_{i}}\})=\sum_{i}p_{i}f^{\#}(\{\delta_{x_{i}}\})=f^{\#}(\{\sum_{i}p_{i}x_{i}\}).

Further on, for
S=\convex\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}
we have
S=\{d_{1}\}\oplus\dots\oplus\{d_{n}\}
and hence

f^{*}(S)=f^{*}(\{d_{1}\})\oplus\dots\oplus f^{*}(\{d_{n}\})=f^{\#}(\{d_{1}\})\oplus\dots\oplus f^{\#}(\{d_{n}\})=\f^{\#}(S)

shows that
f^{*}=f^{\#}
and completes the proof.

The final missing property for the presentation, Lemma
11
, is an easy consequence of the next property that clarifies the definition of
f^{\#}
.

Lemma 19
.

Let
X
be a set and
f\colon X\to CY
a map. Then for all
S
in
CX

f^{\#}(S)=\bigcup\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}(S)=\bigcup_{\Phi\in S}\sum_{u\in\supp(\Phi)}\Phi(u)\cdot f(u).

Proof 4.9
.

The first task is to prove that
f^{\#}(S)=\bigcup\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}(S)
.
Before we proceed, let’s recall all the types. We have
f\colon X\to CY
(and
CY
is the carrier of a convex semilattice), so
f^{\#}\colon CX\to CY
. Also,
f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}\colon\mathcal{D}X\to CY
and hence
\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}\colon\mathcal{P}_{u}\mathcal{D}X\to\mathcal{P}_{u}CY
for
\mathcal{P}_{u}
denoting the unrestricted (and not just finite) powerset. Finally, here
\bigcup\colon\mathcal{P}_{u}\mathcal{P}_{u}\mathcal{D}Y\to\mathcal{P}_{u}\mathcal{D}Y
. Clearly,
CZ\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{u}\mathcal{D}Z
for any set
Z
.

Now, since
S
is convex, by Lemma
16
also
\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}(S)
is convex. Each element of
\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}(S)
is of the form
f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)
for
\Phi\in S
and hence it is in
CY
, i.e., is convex.
By Lemma
18
, we get that
\bigcup\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}(S)
is convex.

Let
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}\in\mathcal{D}X
be such that
S=\convex\{\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}\}
. Clearly,
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}\in S
. Now, we have

\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Psi_{i})\mid i=1,\dots,n\}\subseteq\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\mid\Phi\in S\}

and hence

\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Psi_{i})\mid i=1,\dots,n\}\subseteq\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\mid\Phi\in S\}=\bigcup\overline{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}}(S)

and since the set on the right hand side is convex, as we noted above,

f^{\#}(S)=\bigoplus_{i}f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Psi_{i})=\convex\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Psi_{i})\mid i=1,\dots,n\}\subseteq\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\mid\Phi\in S\},

where the first equality is simply the definition of
f^{\#}
.

For the other inclusion, let
\Phi\in S
. Then
S=\convex\{\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Phi\}
and

f^{\#}(S)=\convex\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Psi_{1}),\dots,f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Psi_{n}),f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\}

by the definition of
f^{\#}
.
Therefore,
f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\subseteq f^{\#}(S)
and since
\Phi
was arbitrary,

\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\mid\Phi\in S\}\subseteq f^{\#}(S).

This proves the first equality of our statement.
For the second equality, note that

\displaystyle f^{\#}(S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup\{f_{\mathcal{D}}^{\#}(\Phi)\mid\Phi\in S\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigcup\{\sum_{u\in\supp(\Phi)}\Phi(u)\cdot f(u)\mid\Phi\in S\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\Phi\in S}\sum_{u\in\supp(\Phi)}\Phi(u)\cdot f(u)

where the equality
(*)
holds as
f^{\#}_{\mathcal{D}}
is convex.

Proof 4.10

(Proof of Lemma
11
)
.

Using Lemma
19
, we immediately get

(\text{\emph{id}}_{CX})^{\#}(S)=\bigcup_{\Phi\in S}\sum_{A\in\supp(\Phi)}\Phi(A)\cdot A=\mu_{X}(S).

5.
Adding termination

So far, we have provided a presentation for the monad
C
which combines probability and nondeterminism. In order to properly model NPLTS, we need a last ingredient: termination. As discussed in Section
3
, termination is given by the monad
\cdot+1
which can always be safely combined with any monad. Following the discussion at the end of Section
3
, the theory
\mathcal{PCS}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP})
presents the monad
C(\cdot+1)
which is the monad of finitely generated non empty convex sets of
sub
distributions.
We call this theory
\mathcal{PCS}
since algebras for this theory are
pointed convex semilattices
, namely convex semilattices with a pointed element denoted by
\star
.

Like for the monad
\mathcal{P}_{ne}
, there exist more than one interesting way of combining
C
with
\cdot+1
. Rather than pointed convex semilattices, one can consider
convex semilattices with bottom
, namely algebras for the theory
\mathcal{CSB}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP}\cup\{(B)\})
obtained by adding
(B)
to
\mathcal{PCS}
. Otherwise, one can add the axiom
(T)
and obtain the theory
\mathcal{CST}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP}\cup\{(T)\})
of
convex semilattices with top
. We denote by
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
and
T_{\mathcal{CST}}
the corresponding monads.

As we will illustrate in Section
6
, particularly relevant for defining trace semantics is the free algebra
\mu\colon MM{\{\bullet\}}\to M\{\bullet\}
generated by a singleton
\{\bullet\}
.
In the next three propositions we respectively identify these algebras for the monad
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
(that is,
C(\cdot+1)
), the monad
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
, and the monad
T_{\mathcal{CST}}
in concrete terms.

Proposition 20
.

\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}=(\mathcal{I},\minmax,+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}},[0,0])
is the free pointed convex semilattice generated by a singleton set
1=\{\bullet\}
.

Proof 5.1
.

Recall that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I}}=(\mathcal{I},\minmax,+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}})
is the convex semilattice of intervals from Section
4
. Then, by interpreting the pointed element
\star
as the interval
[0,0]
we have that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
is a pointed convex semilattice.

Let
2=\{\bullet,\star\}
.
Note that the carrier of the free pointed semilattice generated by
\{\bullet\}
is
C(1+1)=C(2)
.
Recall that
(C(2),\oplus,+_{p})
, where
\oplus
is the convex union and
+_{p}
is the Minkowski sum, is the free convex semilattice generated by
2
.

We next show that
(C(2),\oplus,+_{p})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Indeed
\mathcal{D}(2)
is isomorphic to
[0,1]
: the real number
0
corresponds to
\delta_{\star}
,
1
to
\delta_{\bullet}
and
p\in(0,1)
to
\bullet+_{p}\star
. Furthermore, the non-empty finitely-generated convex subsets of
[0,1]
are the closed intervals.
To conclude, it suffices to see that
\minmax
is
\oplus
on
\mathcal{I}
and
+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}}
is the Minkowski sum.

Proposition 21
.

\maxalg_{B}=([0,1],\max,+_{p},0)
is the free convex semilattice with bottom generated by
1=\{\bullet\}
. ∎

Proof 5.2
.

By Proposition
20
, we know that
C(2)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{I}
.
We show that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
modulo the axiom (B) is isomorphic to
\maxalg_{B}
. We have

\minmax([x,y],[0,0])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B)}}{{=}}[x,y],

for
[x,y]\in\mathcal{I}
.
From

[0,y]=\minmax([x,y],[0,0])=[x,y],

we derive that
[x_{1},y]=[x_{2},y]
for any
x_{1},x_{2},y
. Hence, we define the isomorphism
[x,y]\mapsto y
mapping any interval

[x,y]
to its upper bound
y
.

The interval
[0,0]
is mapped to the bottom element
0
, and the operations are such that:

\displaystyle\minmax([x_{1},y_{1}],[x_{2},y_{2}])

\displaystyle=\minmax([0,y_{1}],[0,y_{2}])

\displaystyle=[0,\max(y_{1},y_{2})]

hence
\minmax([x_{1},y_{1}],[x_{2},y_{2}])\mapsto\max(y_{1},y_{2})
and

[x_{1},y_{1}]+_{p}[x_{2},y_{2}]=[0,y_{1}]+_{p}[0,y_{2}]=[0,y_{1}+_{p}y_{2}]\mapsto y_{1}+_{p}y_{2}.

Proposition 22
.

\minalg_{T}=([0,1],\min,+_{p},0)
is the free convex semilattice with top generated by
1=\{\bullet\}
. ∎

Proof 5.3
.

We show that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
modulo the (T) axiom

\minmax([x,y],[0,0])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(T)}}{{=}}[0,0]

is isomorphic to
\minalg_{T}
.
First, we derive
[x,y_{1}]=[x,y_{2}]
for any
x,y_{1},y_{2}
as follows.
For
x=1
the property trivially holds.
For
x=0
we have

[0,y_{1}]=\minmax([x,y_{1}],[0,0])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(T)}}{{=}}[0,0]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(T)}}{{=}}\minmax([x,y_{2}],[0,0])=[0,y_{2}]

(9)

Finally, for
x\in(0,1)
and
y_{1},y_{2}\geq x
we derive

[x,y_{1}]=[1,1]+_{x}[0,\frac{y_{1}-x}{1-x}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq-star})}}{{=}}[1,1]+_{x}[0,\frac{y_{2}-x}{1-x}]=[x,y_{2}].

Hence, we can now map every interval
[x,y]
to its lower bound
x
.
Then
[0,0]
is mapped to the top element
0
,
and

\minmax([x_{1},y_{1}],[x_{2},y_{2}])=[\min(x_{1},x_{2}),\max({y_{1},y_{2}})]\mapsto\min(x_{1},x_{2})

[x_{1},y_{1}]+_{p}[x_{2},y_{2}]=[x_{1}+_{p}x_{2},y_{1}+_{p}y_{2}]\mapsto x_{1}+_{p}x_{2}.

At this point the reader may wonder what happens when one considers the axioms
(B_{p})
and
(T_{p})
in place of
(B)
and
(T)
. We have already shown at the end of Section
3.4
, that the axiom
(B_{p})
makes the probabilistic structure collapse.
When focussing on the free algebra generated by
\{\bullet\}
, also quotienting by
(T_{p})
is not really interesting: one can show by induction on the terms in
T_{\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T}}(\{\bullet\})
that every term is equal via
E_{NP}\cup\{(T_{p})\}
to either
\bullet
or
\star
or
\bullet\oplus\star
.

So, we have found three interesting ways of combining termination with probability and nondeterminism. Table
2
summarises these theories, their monads, and their algebras.

Theory
(\Sigma,E)

Monad
M

free algebra
\mu_{1}\colon MM1\to M1

\mathcal{PCS}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP})

C(\cdot+1)=T_{\mathcal{PCS}}

\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}=(\mathcal{I},\minmax,+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}},[0,0])

\mathcal{CSB}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP}\cup\{(B)\})

T_{\mathcal{CSB}}

\maxalg_{B}=([0,1],\max,+_{p},0)

\mathcal{CST}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP}\cup\{(T)\})

T_{\mathcal{CST}}

\minalg_{T}=([0,1],\min,+_{p},0)

Table 2.
The theories of pointed convex semilattices, with bottom, and with top.

This completes our exploration of monads and algebras. In the next section, we will commence investigating coalgebras. But first, we show a useful result that illustrates the relationships among the monads encountered so far, where
\mathcal{SB}
and
\mathcal{ST}
are respectively the theory of semilattices with bottom and the theory of semilattices with top.

Lemma 23
.

There exist the following monad maps:

\textstyle{T_{\mathcal{SB}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{B}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{B}}
\scriptstyle{q^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}{(\cdot+1)}}
\textstyle{T_{\mathcal{ST}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{T}}
\textstyle{T_{\mathcal{CSB}}}
\textstyle{C(\cdot+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{B}}
\scriptstyle{q^{T}}
\textstyle{T_{\mathcal{CST}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}}

Moreover: 1. the vertical maps are injective; 2. the diagonal maps
q^{B}\circ\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}
and
q^{T}\circ\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}
are injective; 3. the two squares commute.

Proof 5.4
.

We define
e^{B}\colon T_{\mathcal{SB}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
by
e_{X}([t]_{\mathcal{SB}})=[t]_{\mathcal{CSB}}
for any term
t
with variables in
X
in signature
\Sigma_{N}\cup\Sigma_{T}
, where
[t]_{\mathcal{SB}}
on the left denotes the equivalence class of
t
modulo
E_{N}\cup\{(B)\}
and
[t]_{\mathcal{CSB}}
on the right the equivalence class of
t
modulo
E_{NP}\cup\{(B)\}
. This is justified as every
T_{\mathcal{SB}}
-term is a
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
-term as well. This is easily seen to be a monad map, we need to check well-definedness and injectivity:
t=_{\mathcal{SB}}t^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow t=_{\mathcal{CSB}}t^{\prime}
. Well-definedness, the implication left-to-right, is immediate as the equations of a semilattice with bottom are included in the equations of a convex semilattice with bottom. Assume
t=_{\mathcal{CSB}}t^{\prime}
. Let
\bar{s}
denote the term obtained from a term
s
in
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
by replacing every occurrence of
+_{p}
by
\oplus
. Then we have

s_{1}=_{\mathcal{CSB}}s_{2}\Rightarrow\bar{s}_{1}=_{\mathcal{SB}}\bar{s}_{2}

which is easy to show by checking that it holds for each of the equations.

Now, let
t=t_{1}=_{\mathcal{CSB}}t_{2}\cdots=_{\mathcal{CSB}}t_{n}=t^{\prime}
. Then

t=\bar{t}_{1}=_{\mathcal{SB}}\bar{t}_{2}\cdots=_{\mathcal{SB}}\bar{t}_{n}=t^{\prime}
where the first and last equality hold since
t
and
t^{\prime}
are terms in
\Sigma_{N}\cup\{(B)\}
showing injectivity.

The definition and the proof for
e^{T}\colon T_{\mathcal{ST}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CST}}
are as above but replacing axiom
(B)
by
(T)
.
The maps
\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}{(\cdot+1)}
and
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}
are injective monad maps due to Lemma
1
applied to the injective monad maps
\chi^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}
and
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}
from Lemma
6
.
The horizontal maps are obtained by quotienting by axioms
(B)
and
(T)
the monads
T_{\mathcal{PS}}=\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)
and
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}=C(\cdot+1)
.
Since, for every
\Delta\in\mathcal{D}(X+1)
,
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}_{X}(\Delta)
is a singleton set, the quotients
q^{B}
and
q^{T}
do not affect such set: both
q^{B}\circ\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}
and
q^{T}\circ\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}
are injective.
Commutation of the two squares is immediate.

6.
Coalgebras and Determinisation

In this section, we briefly introduce coalgebra (Section
6.1
) and the generalised determinisation
[
SBBR10
]
construction (Section
6.2
), as well as several examples of transitions systems and automata featuring either nondeterministic or probabilistic behaviour. We present some simple facts and a novel general result (Section
6.3
) that will be useful in Section
7
to prove some key properties for systems featuring –at the same time– nondeterminism and probability. At the end of this section (Section
6.4
), we provide a general notion of trace semantics for transition systems and we illustrate some important examples.

6.1.
Coalgebra

The theory of coalgebra provides an abstract framework for state-based transition systems and automata. A
coalgebra
for a functor
F
in
\mathbf{Sets}
(also called
F
-coalgebra) is a pair
(S,c)
of a state space
S
and a function
c\colon S\to FS

where
F\colon\mathbf{Sets}\rightarrow\mathbf{Sets}
specifies the type of transitions. Sometimes we say the coalgebra
c\colon S\to FS
, meaning the coalgebra
(S,c)
.

A
coalgebra homomorphism
from a coalgebra
(S,c)
to a coalgebra
(T,d)
is a function
h\colon S\to T
that satisfies
d\circ h=Fh\circ c
. Coalgebras for a functor
F
and their coalgebra homomorphisms form a category, denoted by
\CoAlg{(F)}
.

The final object in
\CoAlg{(F)}
, when it exists, is the
final
F
-coalgebra
. We write
\smash{\zeta\colon Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}FZ}
for the final
F
-coalgebra. For every coalgebra
c\colon S\to FS
, there is a unique homomorphism
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{c}
to the final one, the
final coalgebra map
, making the diagram below commute:

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\,\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{c}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{FS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{c}}
\textstyle{FZ}

The
final coalgebra semantics

\sim
is the kernel of the final coalgebra map, i.e., two states
s
and
t
are equivalent in the final coalgebra semantics iff
\llbracket s\rrbracket_{c}=\llbracket t\rrbracket_{c}
.

Even without a final coalgebra, coalgebras over a concrete category are equipped with a generic behavioural equivalence.
Let
(S,c)
be an
F
-coalgebra on
\mathbf{Sets}
. An equivalence relation
R\subseteq S\times S
is a kernel bisimulation (synonymously, a cocongruence)
[
Sta11
,
Kur00
,
Wol00
]
if it is the kernel of a homomorphism, i.e.,
R=\ker h=\{(s,t)\in S\times S\mid h(s)=h(t)\}
for some coalgebra homomorphism
h\colon(S,c)\to(T,d)
to some
F
-coalgebra
(T,d)
. Two states
s,t
of a coalgebra are
behaviourally equivalent
(notation:
s\approx t
) iff there is a kernel bisimulation
R
with
(s,t)\in R
. If a final coalgebra exists, then the behavioural equivalence and the final coalgebra semantics coincide, i.e.,
\approx\,\,=\,\,\sim
.

The following are well-known examples of
F
-coalgebras that will be useful later on:

(1)

Labelled transition systems, LTS, are coalgebras for the functor
F=(\mathcal{P}(\cdot))^{A}
. Behavioural equivalence coincides with strong bisimilarity.

(2)

Nondeterministic automata, NA, are coalgebras for
F=2\times(\mathcal{P}(\cdot))^{A}
where
2=\{0,1\}
is needed to differentiate whether a state is accepting or not.

(3)

Deterministic automata, DA, are coalgebras for
F=2\times(\cdot)^{A}
. The final coalgebra is carried by the set of all languages
2^{A^{*}}
.

(4)

Moore automata, MA, are a slight generalisation of deterministic automata with observations
O
: they are coalgebras for
F=O\times(\cdot)^{A}
. The final coalgebra is carried by the set of all
O
-valued languages
O^{A^{*}}
.

(5)

Reactive probabilistic labelled transition systems, RPLTS, are coalgebras for
F=(\mathcal{D}(\cdot)+1)^{A}
. Behavioural equivalence coincides with Larsen-Skou bisimilarity
[
LS91
]
.

(6)

(Rabin) Probabilistic automata
[
Rab63
]
, PA, are coalgebras for
F=[0,1]\times\mathcal{D}(\cdot)^{A}
.

The following definition generalises the examples above.

{defi}

[Systems and Automata with
M
-effects]
Let
M
be a monad and
O
be a set. We call an
M^{A}
-coalgebra a
system with
M
-effects
, and we call an
O\times M^{A}
-coalgebra an
automaton with
M
-effects and observations in
O
.
We write
c=\langle o,t\rangle
for an automaton with
M
-effects and observations in
O
, where
o\colon X\to O
is the observation map assigning observations to states, and
t\colon X\to(MX)^{A}
is the transition structure.

For instance, LTS are systems with
\mathcal{P}
-effects while NA are automata with
\mathcal{P}
-effects and observations in
2
. Similarly, RPLTS are systems with
\mathcal{D}+1
-effects, while PA are automata with
\mathcal{D}
-effects and observations in
[0,1]
. Both DA and MA are automata with no effects (
M=\Id
) and observations in
2
and
O
, respectively.

We write
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}m
for
t(x)(a)=m
with
a\in A,x\in X,m\in MX
in a system or automaton with
M
-effects. We also write
x\downarrow{o_{x}}
for
o(x)=o_{x}
with
o_{x}\in O
. For an LTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
we also write, as usual,
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}y
for
y\in t(x)(a)
and
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\not\to}}
if
t(x)(a)=\emptyset
; for an RPLTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}
, we may also write
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to_{p}}}y
for
t(x)(a)(y)=p
and again
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\not\to}}
if
t(x)(a)=\star
.
Note that in all our examples of systems and automata there is an implicit finite branching property ensured by the use of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{D}
involving only finite subsets and finitely supported distributions.

6.2.
Determinising Automata with
M
-effects and Observations in
O

The construction of generalised determinisation was originally discovered in
[
SBBR10
,
Bar04
]
. It enables us to obtain trace semantics for coalgebras of type
c\colon X\to FMX
where
F
is a functor and
M
a monad. The result is a determinised
F
-coalgebra
c^{\#}\colon MX\to FMX
and the semantics is derived from behavioural equivalence for
F
-coalgebras.

Let
c\colon X\to FMX
be a coalgebra and
\lambda\colon MF\Rightarrow FM
a functor distributive law. Then the determinisation is the
F
-coalgebra

c^{\sharp}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{FMX}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

(10)

It is easy to show that
c^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\eta=c
which justifies the notation
c^{\sharp}
:
the carrier
MX
carries the
M
-algebra
\mu_{X}\colon MMX\to MX
, the free
M
-algebra generated by
X
,
FMX
carries the
M
-algebra
F\mu\mathrel{\circ}\lambda\colon MFMX\to FMX
, and
c^{\sharp}
is the unique extension of
c
to a homomorphism from the free
M
-algebra
(MX,\mu)
to the
M
-algebra
(FMX,F\mu\mathrel{\circ}\lambda)
.

In this paper, we only consider determinisation of automata with
M
-effects and observations in
O
, namely,
FM
-coalgebras for
the Moore-automata functor
F=O\times(\cdot)^{A}
, where
O
is some set of observations.
The following proposition shows that determinising automata with
M
-effects and observations in
O
is always possible when the observations carry an
M
-algebra
[

,
JSS15
]
.

Proposition 24
.

For an Eilenberg-Moore algebra
a\colon MO\to O
, for
F=O\times(\cdot)^{A}
and any monad
M
on
\mathbf{Sets}
there is a canonical distributive law
\lambda\colon MF\Rightarrow FM
given by
​​

\textstyle{{M\big(O\!\times\!X^{A}\big)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\langle M\pi_{1},M\pi_{2}\rangle\!}
\textstyle{{MO\!\times\!M(X^{A})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a\times\text{\emph{st}}}
\textstyle{{O\!\times\!(MX)^{A}}}

where
st
is the map
\text{\emph{st}}\colon M(X^{A})\to(MX)^{A}
defined, for all labels
a\in A
, by
\text{\emph{st}}(\varphi)(a)=M\mbox{\sl ev}_{a}(\varphi)
with
\mbox{\sl ev}_{a}\colon X^{A}\to X
the evaluation map given as
\mbox{\sl ev}_{a}(\varphi)=\varphi(a)
. ∎

As a consequence, we can determinise
c=\langle o,t\rangle\colon X\to O\times(MX)^{A}
to
c^{\sharp}=\langle o^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle
where

\displaystyle o^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\textstyle{MX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Mo}
\textstyle{MO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{O}
and

\displaystyle t^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\textstyle{MX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Mt}
\textstyle{M(MX)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
st
\textstyle{(MMX)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}^{A}}
\textstyle{(MX)^{A}}
.

The final
F
-coalgebra, for
F=O\times(\cdot)^{A}
, is carried by the set
O^{A^{*}}
of
O
-valued languages over alphabet
A
, i.e., functions
\varphi\colon A^{*}\to O
. The final coalgebra is

\zeta=\langle\epsilon,\dder\rangle\colon O^{A^{*}}\to O\times(O^{A^{*}})^{A}

where for all
\varphi\in O^{A^{*}}
,
\epsilon\colon O^{A^{*}}\to O
is defined as
\epsilon(\varphi)=\varphi(\varepsilon)
and
\dder\colon O^{A^{*}}\to(O^{A^{*}})^{A}
as
\dder(\varphi)(a)(w)=\varphi(aw)
.

The morphism into the final coalgebra
[\![\cdot]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\colon MX\to O^{A^{*}}

]
) for all

and
