[\![m]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}(aw)&=&[\![t^{\sharp}(m)(a)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}(w)\end{array}\end{array}

(13)

{defi}

[Language semantics for automata]Let
(M,\eta,\mu)
be a monad and
c=\langle o,t\rangle\colon X\to O\times MX^{A}
be an automaton with
M
-effects and observations in
O
. Let
a\colon MO\to O
be a
M
-algebra. Two states
x,y\in X
are
language equivalent
, written
x\equiv y
iff

[\![\eta(x)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}=[\![\eta(y)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}

where
c^{\sharp}
is the determinisation w.r.t.
a
of
c=\langle o,t\rangle
.

The commuting diagram on the left in Eq.
13
summarises our setting. An important observation is that
O^{A^{*}}
carries an
M
-algebra defined as the point-wise extension of
a\colon MO\to O
. Similarly,
O\times(O^{A^{*}})^{A}
also carries an
M
-algebra and the final
F
-coalgebra
\zeta=\langle\epsilon,der\rangle
is actually an
M
-algebra homomorphism. Recall that also
c^{\sharp}=\langle o^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle\colon MX\to O\times MX^{A}
is at the same time an
F
-coalgebra and an
M
-algebra homomorphism: both
\langle\epsilon,der\rangle
and
\langle o^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle
are indeed examples of
\lambda
-bialgebras

[
DP97
,
Kli11
]
. Most importantly, the unique coalgebra morphism
[\![\cdot]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\colon MX\to O^{A^{*}}
is also an
M
-algebra homomorphism
4
4

4

From a more abstract perspective, the distributive law
\lambda
allows for lifting the functor
F
to
\EM(M)
as well as the rightmost commuting square in (
13
): all objects are
M
-algebras and all arrows are
M
-algebra homomorphism. We refer the interested reader to
[
Kli11
]
for a gentle introduction to the subject.
. The latter entails the first item of the following.

{thmC}

[
[
SBBR10
,
BPPR17
]
]
The following hold for any coalgebra
c\colon X{\to}FMX
and its determinisation
c^{\sharp}\colon MX{\to}FMX
:

(1)

Behavioural equivalence for
(MX,c^{\sharp})
is a congruence for the algebraic structure of
M
.

(2)

Behavioural equivalence for
(X,c)
implies language equivalence.

(3)

Up-to context is a compatible
[
PS12
]
proof technique.

The second item will be exploited in Section
7
to show that convex bisimilarity implies trace equivalence for NPLTS, while the third item will be fundamental in Section
7.2
to prove the soundness of an effective proof technique. Below we give a more concrete description of the overall construction by relying on a presentation
(\Sigma,E)
for the monad
M
.

For an
n
-ary operation symbol
f\in\Sigma
and a
(\Sigma,E)
-algebra
\mathbb{A}=(A,\Sigma_{A})
we write
f^{A}
for the
n
-ary operation on
A
that is the interpretation of
f
. We have
that
c^{\sharp}=\langle o^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle
can be defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{ll}o^{\sharp}(x)=o(x)&o^{\sharp}(f^{MX}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}))=f^{O}(o^{\sharp}(s_{1}),\dots,o^{\sharp}(s_{n}))\\
t^{\sharp}(x)=t(x)&t^{\sharp}(f^{MX}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}))(a)=f^{MX}(t^{\sharp}(s_{1})(a),\dots,t^{\sharp}(s_{n})(a))\end{array}

(14)

Remark 25
.

Technically, the above definition is telling us that
o^{\sharp}\colon MX\to O
is the unique
M
-algebra homomorphism extending the function
o\colon X\to O
and similarly for
t^{\sharp}\colon MX\to MX^{A}
. Such a definition (of a function on
E
-equivalence classes of
\Sigma
-terms) can also be seen as arising from an inductively defined function on
\Sigma
-terms. To be precise, it is important to recall that defining a function over equivalence classes based on representatives always requires to check whether the function is well-defined, in the sense that it is independent of the choice of a representative. Fortunately enough, monad properties guarantee that
o^{\sharp}\colon MX\to O
and
t^{\sharp}\colon MX\to MX^{A}
are well-defined in this respect
5
5

5

It is enough to observe that (a) the function
\hat{o}^{\sharp}\colon T_{\Sigma}X\to O
defined inductively on terms in
T_{\Sigma}X
is the unique
\Sigma
-algebra homomorphism extending
o
;
(b) that there is a monad quotient
q^{E}\colon T_{\Sigma}\Rightarrow M
and (c)
q^{E}_{X}\colon T_{\Sigma}X\to MX
is a
\Sigma
-algebra homomorphism. By uniqueness,
\hat{o}^{\sharp}=o^{\sharp}\circ q^{E}_{X}
.
and thus (
14
) can effectively be thought of as an inductive definition on terms. In the remainder of this paper, we will instantiate (
14
) to several examples in this way, without mentioning again that
o^{\sharp}
and
t^{\sharp}
are well-defined.

The
M
-algebra structure over the final
F
-coalgebra is given for
\varphi_{i}\in O^{A^{*}}
and
w\in A^{*}
as

\displaystyle f^{O^{A^{*}}}(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})(w)=f^{O}(\varphi_{1}(w),\dots,\varphi_{n}(w))

The fact that
[\![\cdot]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\colon MX\to O^{A^{*}}
is an
M
-algebra homomorphism just means that

\displaystyle[\![f^{MX}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}=f^{O^{A^{*}}}([\![s_{1}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}},\dots[\![s_{n}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}})

(15)

for all
n
-ary operator
f
in
\Sigma
. Clearly this fact immediately entails that
\approx
is a congruence w.r.t. the operations in
\Sigma
.

{exa}

[Determinisation of Nondeterministic Automata]
Applying this construction to
F=2\times(\cdot)^{A}
and
M=\mathcal{P}
, one transforms
c\colon X\to 2\times(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
into
c^{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{P}X\to 2\times(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
. The former is a nondeterministic automaton and the latter is a deterministic automaton which has
\mathcal{P}X
as states space. The set of all languages
2^{A^{*}}
(seen as functions in
2=\{0,1\}
) carries the final
F
-coalgebra
\langle\epsilon,\dder\rangle\colon 2^{A^{*}}\to 2\times(2^{A^{*}})^{A}
which is exactly the emptiness operation and the derivatives operations by Brzozowski
[
Brz64
]
. The final coalgebra morphism
[\![\cdot]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\colon\mathcal{P}X\to 2^{A^{*}}
maps each state of the determinised automaton into the language that it accepts.

In
[
SBBR10
]
, see also
[
JSS15
]
, it is shown that, using the distributive law from Proposition
24
, as
2=\mathcal{P}1
is the carrier of the free
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
\mu_{1}\colon\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}1\to\mathcal{P}1
, this amounts exactly to the standard determinisation from automata theory and justifies the term
generalised determinisation
.

Recalling that
\mathcal{P}
is presented by the algebraic theory of semilattices with bottom gives us a more concrete understanding. The set of observations
2=\{0,1\}
carries the semilattice with bottom
0\sqsubseteq 1
with supremum operation denoted by
\sqcup
. The interpretation of
\oplus
and
\star
in this algebra are defined for all
b_{1},b_{2}\in 2
as

b_{1}\oplus^{2}b_{2}=b_{1}\sqcup b_{2}\qquad\star^{2}=0\text{.}

By instantiating (
14
), one has that the determinisation
c^{\sharp}=\langle o^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle\colon\mathcal{P}X\to 2\times(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
is defined inductively for all
S\in\mathcal{P}X
and
a\in A
as

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}o^{\sharp}(S)&\!\!=&\begin{cases}o(x)&\text{ if }S=\eta(x)\text{;}\\
\star^{2}&\text{ if }S=\star^{\mathcal{P}X}\text{;}\\
o^{\sharp}(S_{1})\oplus^{2}o^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\oplus^{\mathcal{P}X}S_{2}\text{;}\\
\end{cases}\\
&\!\!=&\begin{cases}o(x)&\text{ if }S=\{x\}\text{;}\\
0&\text{ if }S=\emptyset\text{;}\\
o^{\sharp}(S_{1})\sqcup o^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}\text{;}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}t^{\sharp}(S)(a)&\!\!=&\begin{cases}t(x)(a)&\text{ if }S=\eta(x)\text{;}\\
\star^{\mathcal{P}X}&\text{ if }S=\star^{\mathcal{P}X}\text{;}\\
t^{\sharp}(S_{1})\oplus^{\mathcal{P}X}t^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\oplus^{\mathcal{P}X}S_{2}\text{;}\\
\end{cases}\\
&\!\!=&\begin{cases}t(x)(a)&\text{ if }S=\{x\}\text{;}\\
\emptyset&\text{ if }S=\emptyset\text{;}\\
t^{\sharp}(S_{1})\cup t^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}\text{;}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

since
\oplus^{\mathcal{P}X}
is union of subsets and
\star^{\mathcal{P}X}
is the empty set. We have seen an example of this construction in Section
1
.

The semilattice with bottom over the final
F
-coalgebra
2^{A^{*}}
is defined as the pointwise extension of the semilattice over
2
, that is, for all
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in 2^{A^{*}}
and
w\in A^{*}

(\varphi_{1}\oplus^{2^{A^{*}}}\varphi_{2})(w)=\varphi_{1}(w)\sqcup\varphi_{2}(w)\qquad\star^{2^{A^{*}}}(w)=0

Observe that
\oplus^{2^{A^{*}}}
is just the union of languages and
\star^{2^{A^{*}}}
is the empty language. By instantiating (
15
), one has that

[\![S_{1}\oplus^{\mathcal{P}X}S_{2}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}=[\![S_{1}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\oplus^{2^{A^{*}}}[\![S_{2}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\qquad[\![\star^{\mathcal{P}X}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}=\star^{2^{A^{*}}}

meaning that (a) the language accepted by the union of two sets of states is exactly the union of the languages accepted by the two sets and (b) the empty set accepts the empty language. This immediately entails the first item of Theorem
6.2
. The second item, intuitively corresponds to the usual fact that bisimilarity implies language equivalence. The third item allows for exploiting bisimulations up-to
\oplus
and, consequently, for an efficient algorithm to check language equivalence
[
BP13
]
.

{exa}

[Determinisation of Probabilistic Automata]
Applying the construction for
F=[0,1]\times(\cdot)^{A}
and
M=\mathcal{D}
, one transforms
c\colon X\to[0,1]\times(\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
into
c^{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{D}X\to[0,1]\times(\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
. The former is a probabilistic automaton and the latter is a Moore automaton with set of observations
O=[0,1]
. The set of all
[0,1]
-valued languages
[0,1]^{A^{*}}
carries the final
F
-coalgebra.
Since
[0,1]=\mathcal{D}(2)
, the set
[0,1]
carries the
\mathcal{D}
-algebra
\mu_{2}\colon\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}(2)\to\mathcal{D}(2)
which amounts to taking convex combinations in
[0,1]
. By exploiting this algebra for the distributive law in Proposition
24
, one obtains a final coalgebra semantics that coincides with probabilistic language equivalence of
[
Rab63
]
(see
[
SBBR10
]
).

Next we illustrate more concretely this construction by relying on the presentation of
\mathcal{D}
as the algebraic theory of convex algebras. For
p\in[0,1]
, the interpretation of the operation
+_{p}
in
[0,1]
is as expected:
q_{1}+_{p}^{[0,1]}q_{2}=p\cdot q_{1}+(1-p)\cdot q_{2}
.
By instantiating (
14
), one has that the determinisation
c^{\sharp}=\langle o^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle\colon\mathcal{D}X\to[0,1]\times(\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
is defined inductively for all
\Delta\in\mathcal{D}X
and
a\in A
as

\begin{array}[]{lcl}o^{\sharp}(\Delta)&=&\begin{cases}o(x)&\text{ if }\Delta=\eta(x)=\delta_{x}\text{;}\\
o^{\sharp}(\Delta_{1})+_{p}^{[0,1]}o^{\sharp}(\Delta_{2})&\text{ if }\Delta=\Delta_{1}+_{p}^{\mathcal{D}X}\Delta_{2}\text{;}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}t^{\sharp}(\Delta)(a)&=&\begin{cases}t(x)(a)&\text{ if }\Delta=\eta(x)=\delta_{x}\text{;}\\
t^{\sharp}(\Delta_{1})+_{p}^{\mathcal{D}X}t^{\sharp}(\Delta_{2})&\text{ if }\Delta=\Delta_{1}+_{p}^{\mathcal{D}X}\Delta_{2}\text{;}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

We have seen an example of this construction in Section
1
.
The convex algebra over the final coalgebra
[0,1]^{A^{*}}
is defined as the pointwise extension of the algebra over
[0,1]
, that is

(\varphi_{1}+_{p}^{[0,1]^{A^{*}}}\varphi_{2})(w)=\varphi_{1}(w)+_{p}^{[0,1]}\varphi_{2}(w)

for
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in[0,1]^{A^{*}}
and
w\in A^{*}
. By instantiating (
15
), one has that

[\![\Delta_{1}+_{p}^{\mathcal{D}X}\Delta_{2}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}=[\![\Delta_{1}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}+_{p}^{[0,1]^{A^{*}}}[\![\Delta_{2}]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}\text{.}

We will apply similar methods to obtain languages and language equivalence for automata with both nondeterminism and probability in the rest of the paper.

6.3.
Invariance of the Semantics

We next state a theorem that guarantees invariance of the language semantics for automata with
M
-effects and observations in
O
, under controlled changes of the monad or the algebra of observations.

Theorem 26

(Invariance Theorem)
.

Let
(M,\eta,\mu)
be a monad and
a\colon MO\to O
an
M
-algebra. Let
c=\langle o,t\rangle\colon X\to O\times(MX)^{A}
be an automaton with
M
-effects and observations in
O
and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket\colon MX\to O^{A^{*}}
be the semantic map induced by the generalised determinisation w.r.t.
a
, i.e.,
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket=\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{c^{\sharp}}

(1)

Transitions:
Let
(\hat{M},\hat{\eta},\hat{\mu})
be a monad and
\sigma\colon M\Rightarrow\hat{M}
a monad map. Let
\hat{a}\colon\hat{M}O\to O
be an
\hat{M}
-algebra. Consider the coalgebra

\hat{c}=\langle o,\hat{t}\,\rangle=\langle o,\sigma_{X}^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t\rangle\colon X\to O\times(\hat{M}X)^{A}

, then

.

Both squares on the left trivially commute by definition. To prove that also the square on the right commutes, it is enough to show that
h^{A^{*}}\colon O^{A^{*}}\to\hat{O}^{A^{*}}
coincides with the unique coalgebra morphism
[\![\cdot]\!]_{d}
from the coalgebra

d=(h\times id_{(O^{A^{*}})^{A}})\circ\zeta\colon O^{A^{*}}\to{\hat{O}}\times(O^{A^{*}})^{A}

to the final

-coalgebra
\hat{\zeta}
.

From the inductive definition of

, see Eq.
13
, we get
[\![\varphi]\!]_{d}=\lambda w\in A^{*}.\,h\mathrel{\circ}\epsilon((\varphi)_{w})

which easily leads to
[\![\varphi]\!]_{d}=h\mathrel{\circ}\varphi=h^{A^{*}}(\varphi)
.

Now observe that
h\mathrel{\circ}o^{\sharp}
is equal to

, since

is an algebra morphism.

From this observation and the commuting of the above diagram, it follows that
h^{A^{*}}\mathrel{\circ}[\![\cdot]\!]
is the unique coalgebra morphism from
\hat{c}^{\sharp}=\langle(h\mathrel{\circ}o)^{\sharp},t^{\sharp}\rangle
to the final

-coalgebra, and hence it equals
\hat{[\![\cdot]\!]}
.

Whenever the monad
M
has a presentation
(\Sigma,E)
, the first part of the theorem above provides a convenient technique that allows one to work with syntactic terms in
T_{\Sigma}
and forget about the axioms in
E
. Let
q^{E}\colon T_{\Sigma}\Rightarrow M
be the monad morphism quotienting
T_{\Sigma}
by the axioms in
E
and let
r_{X}\colon MX\to T_{\Sigma}X
be a right inverse of
q^{E}_{X}
, namely
q^{E}_{X}\circ r_{X}=id_{MX}
. Now, the automaton with
M
-effects
c=\langle o,t\rangle\colon X\to O\times(MX)^{A}
can be translated into an automaton with
T_{\Sigma}
-effects by taking

c^{\prime}=\langle o,t^{\prime}\,\rangle=\langle o,r_{X}^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t\rangle\colon X\to O\times(T_{\Sigma}X)^{A}\text{.}

Similarly the
M
-algebra of observation
a\colon MO\to O
gives rise to the
T_{\Sigma}
-algebra

a^{\prime}=a\mathrel{\circ}q^{E}_{O}\colon T_{\Sigma}O\to O\text{.}

Now, rather than determinise
c
with respect to
a
, one can determinise
c^{\prime}
w.r.t.
a^{\prime}
and, by virtue of Theorem
26
.1, the semantics does not change.

Corollary 27
.

Let
M
be a monad with presentation
(\Sigma,E)
.
Let
c=\langle o,t\rangle\colon X\to O\times(MX)^{A}
and
c^{\prime}=\langle o,t^{\prime}\rangle\colon X\to O\times(T_{\Sigma}X)^{A}
be such that
q^{E}_{X}\circ t^{\prime}=t
. Let
a\colon MO\to O
be an
M
-algebra.
Let
[\![\cdot]\!]
and
[\![\cdot]\!]^{\prime}
be the semantics maps induced by the determinisation of
c
w.r.t.
a
and, respectively,
c^{\prime}
w.r.t.
a^{\prime}=a\mathrel{\circ}q^{E}_{O}
. Let
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
be the units of
M
and
T_{\Sigma}
.
Then,
[\![\cdot]\!]\mathrel{\circ}\eta_{X}=[\![\cdot]\!]\mathrel{\circ}\eta_{X}^{\prime}
.

Proof 6.2
.

The property follows immediately by Theorem
26
.1, but the reader has to be careful not to be mislead: Theorem
26
.1 cannot be applied to
r_{X}
, since this is not a monad map (actually, it is not even a natural transformation). The monad map
\sigma\colon M\Rightarrow\hat{M}
in the statement of the theorem is the quotient
q^{E}:T_{\Sigma}\Rightarrow M
in the corollary, indeed the monads
M
and
\hat{M}
in the theorem correspond to the monads
T_{\Sigma}
and
M
in the corollary, respectively.
Similarly, the automaton with
M
-effects
c
in the theorem is
c^{\prime}
in the corollary, while
\hat{c}
is
c
in the corollary. The algebras
\hat{a}
and
a
in the theorem correspond to
a
and
a^{\prime}
in the corollary, respectively.

Determinising
c^{\prime}
rather than
c
, makes our approach similar to processes calculi. Indeed,
in
c^{\prime\sharp}=\langle o^{\prime\sharp},t^{\prime\sharp}\rangle\colon T_{\Sigma}X\to O\times T_{\Sigma}X^{A}
, states are syntactic terms rather than elements in
MX
, e.g., sets, distributions, …. The output and transition functions
o^{\prime\sharp},t^{\prime\sharp}
can be defined
by means of GSOS rules. For each
n
-ary operator
f
in
\Sigma
, we have one rule for output and one rule per action
a\in A
for transitions:

\frac{s_{i}\downarrow o_{i}\quad i\in 1\dots n}{f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\downarrow f^{O}(o_{1},\dots,o_{n})}\qquad\frac{s_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}s_{i}^{\prime}\quad i\in 1\dots n}{f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}f(s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{n}^{\prime})}

(17)

Here
f^{O}
is the interpretation of
f
in the algebra of observation
a\colon MO\to O
. For each
x\in X
, we have the following axioms:

\frac{-}{x\downarrow o(x)}\qquad\frac{-}{x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}t^{\prime}(x)(a)}

(18)

Remark 28
.

The rules in (
17
) and (
18
) are equivalent — modulo the axioms in
E
— to the definition of
o^{\sharp}
and
t^{\sharp}
provided by (
14
)
6
6

6

The reader can easily check by induction that
o^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}q^{E}_{X}=o^{\prime\sharp}
and that
t^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}q^{E}_{X}=(q^{E}_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t^{\prime\sharp}
.
. However, the two rules above provide a specification which is closer to (the standard way of giving) the structured operational semantics via so-called GSOS rules where states are syntactic terms and transitions are derived by the structure of terms. A notable difference, beyond the outputs, is that the transitions specified by (
17
) and (
18
) are deterministic:
s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}s^{\prime}
means that
t^{\prime\sharp}(s)(a)=s^{\prime}
and not that
s^{\prime}\in t^{\prime}(s)(a)
as common in structured operational semantics.

The connection between GSOS rules and bialgebras was first identified in the seminal work by Turi and Plotkin
[
DP97
]
: they show that GSOS rules are in one-to-one correspondence with certain natural transformations that, in turn, give rise to distributive laws of type
\lambda\colon MF\Rightarrow FM
, see Section
3.3
, for
F=\mathcal{P}(\cdot)^{A}
and
M
the term monad
T_{\Sigma}
for some signature
\Sigma
. The rules in (
17
) instead can be regarded as a natural transformation
\rho\colon\Sigma F\Rightarrow F\Sigma
, for
\Sigma
the functor corresponding to a signature and
F=O\times(\cdot)^{A}
. In
[
Kli11
]
, it is shown that natural transformations of this kind, that are named there “simple distributive laws”, give rise to a distributive law of type
\lambda\colon T_{\Sigma}F\Rightarrow FT_{\Sigma}
. In our case, the rules in (
17
) give rise exactly to the distributive law from Proposition
24
for
M=T_{\Sigma}
.

6.4.
From Systems to Automata and the Role of Termination

So far, we have seen how language semantics arises by determinisation of automata. However, our initial interest concerns trace semantics. Trace semantics is closely related to language semantics, but it concerns systems that lack the notion of observation. At the same time, dealing with automata, i.e., having observations, is crucial for determinisation. In this section, we discuss the move from systems to automata that enables defining trace semantics with help of language semantics. The intuition can easily be explained in the case of LTS: Create an automaton from an LTS by making every state accepting. Then trace equivalence for the LTS is language equivalence for the created automaton.

More precisely, starting from an LTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
, we can add observations in
2=\mathcal{P}1
in the simplest possible way, making every state an accepting state:

o=(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}1=2)

and determinise the NA
\langle o,t\rangle\colon X\to 2\times(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
w.r.t. the algebra
\mu_{1}\colon\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}1\to\mathcal{P}1
. The induced language semantics on the state space
X
is the standard trace semantics for LTS
[
BBC+16
]
.

This same approach can be applied in the case of any system with
M
-effects
t\colon X\to(MX)^{A}
. We can add observations in
O=M1
by

o=(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M1),

(19)

determinise the automaton
\langle o,t\rangle
with
M
-effects using the free algebra on
M1
, and consider the induced language semantics.
This idea is summarised in the following definition.

{defi}

[Trace semantics for systems with
M
-effects]
Let
(M,\eta,\mu)
be a monad and
t\colon X\to MX^{A}
be a system with
M
-effects. Two states
x,y\in X
are trace equivalent, written
x\equiv y
iff

[\![\eta(x)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}=[\![\eta(y)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}

where
c^{\sharp}
is the determinisation w.r.t.
\mu_{1}\colon MM1\to M1
of
c=\langle o,t\rangle\colon MX\to M1\times MX^{A}
for
o
defined as in (
19
).

However, not everything is settled yet, as shown by the following example — the way we model systems, i.e., the choice of the monad involved, may make a huge difference in the semantics that we obtain.

{exa}

Let us apply Definition
6.4
to an RPLTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}
, namely a system with
\mathcal{D}+1
-effects. Recall the monad
\mathcal{D}+1
from Example
3.3
. By taking as sets of observations
O=M1
, one would obtain that
O=\mathcal{D}(1)+1
which is isomorphic to
2
. Therefore, the final coalgebra would be
2^{A^{*}}
making impossible to distinguish probabilistic behaviours.

The problem is not in the choice of observations but already in the monad
\mathcal{D}+1
. Indeed, even by allowing different observations, determinising w.r.t. such monad would lead a too coarse semantics.
Consider for instance the RPLTS in the top of Figure
2
. The states
x
and
y
should not be trace equivalent, since
x
has probability
\frac{1}{2}
of performing trace
ab
, and
y
has probability
\frac{1}{4}
of performing trace
ab
.
Let us look at what happens, however, if we determinise this system with respect to the monad
\mathcal{D}+1
.
The determinised transition function
t^{\sharp}

will give us states in
\mathcal{D}X+1
, i.e., states that are either full distributions or the element
\star\in 1
and we have

t^{\sharp}(x)(a)=x_{1}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2}\qquad t^{\sharp}(y)(a)=y_{1}+_{\frac{1}{4}}y_{2}

However,

\displaystyle t^{\sharp}(x_{1}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2})(b)=t(x_{1})(b)+_{\frac{1}{2}}t(x_{2})(b)=\star

\displaystyle t^{\sharp}(y_{1}+_{\frac{1}{4}}y_{2})(b)=t(y_{1})(b)+_{\frac{1}{4}}t(y_{2})(b)=\star

Hence, whatever
(\mathcal{D}+1)
-algebra of observation we take, these states in the determinised system will return the same observation, i.e.,
o^{\sharp}(x)(ab)=o^{\sharp}(y)(ab)
.
As a consequence,
x
and
y
will be equivalent.

x
\Delta
x_{1}

The example above suggests that the general trace equivalence given in Definition
6.4
when dealing with systems with effects in a monad of shape
M+1
might not provide the best results. Indeed, the state space of the determinised system for such a monad would always be of the shape
MX+1
, intuitively either an
M
-combination of states in
X
or
\star\in 1
. The solution that we propose here is to work with the monad
M(\cdot+1)
rather than
M+1
. In order to do this it is enough to recall the morphism
\iota\colon M+1\to M(\cdot+1)
from (
1
), transform any system
t\colon X\to(MX+1)^{A}
into

\bar{t}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.53471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.60066pt\raise 5.15279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15279pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 61.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(MX+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.15034pt\raise 6.72002pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.71446pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota^{A}_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 172.90965pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 142.90965pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 172.90965pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(M(X+1))^{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(20)

and then adopt Definition
6.4
.
With this recipe, we can recover the appropriate definition of trace equivalence for RPLTS.

{exa}
[Trace semantics for RPLTS]
We first turn an RPLTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}
into a system with
\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)
-effects by postcomposing with
\iota^{A}
, as in (
20
). Observe that the monad map
\iota\colon\mathcal{D}+1\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)
only changes the type by embedding distributions into subdistributions and regards the unique element of
1
as the empty subdistribution (namely the one mapping everything to
0
).

Now, we can apply Definition
6.4
. The set of observation is
[0,1]=\mathcal{D}(1+1)
equipped with the free
\mathcal{D}(\cdot+1)
-algebra generated by
1
. The observation function
o\colon X\to[0,1]
maps every state
x\in X
into the element
1\in[0,1]
. The function
\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{c^{\sharp}}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\colon X\to[0,1]^{A^{*}}
obtained via the generalised determinisation of
c=\langle o,\bar{t}\rangle
assigns to each state
x\in X
and trace
w\in A^{*}
the probability of reaching from
x
any other state via
w
. We write
\equiv^{RP}
for the induced trace equivalence. A similar construction has been used in
[
FZ14
,
YJZ17
]
.

since
[\![\eta(x)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}(ab)=\frac{1}{2}
and
[\![\eta(y)]\!]_{c^{\sharp}}(ab)=\frac{1}{4}
.

For LTS, we have already seen that Definition
6.4
leads to the standard notion of trace semantics. However, by recalling that
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{ne}+1
, one could first transform an LTS into a systems with
\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)
-effects by (
20
) and then use Definition
6.4
. The following example illustrates the alternative trace equivalence obtained in this way.

{exa}

[May/Must trace semantics for LTS]
Given an LTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
, we define
\bar{t}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}_{ne}(X+1)^{A}
as in (
20
) by recalling that
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{ne}+1
. The embedding
\iota_{X}\colon\mathcal{P}X\to\mathcal{P}_{ne}(X+1)
maps the empty set
\emptyset\in\mathcal{P}X
into the singleton
\{\star\}
, where
\star
is the unique element of
1
, and any nonempty subset to itself.

To obtain the algebra of observations we take the free
\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)
-algebra generated by the singleton set
1=\{\bullet\}
. This is the semilattice

\textstyle{\{\bullet,\star\}}
\textstyle{\{\bullet\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{\star\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(21)

with point
\{\star\}
. Hereafter we refer to this as
3
. The observation function
o\colon X\to 3
maps every state
x\in X
into the element
\{\bullet\}\in 3
. We rely on a
testing scenario
to give an intuition of the semantic map
\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{c^{\sharp}}\mathrel{\circ}\eta\colon X\to 3^{A^{*}}
obtained via the generalised determinisation of
c=\langle o,\bar{t}\rangle
: words
w\in A^{*}
can be thought as tests to be performed on each state
x\in X
of the original LTS; the outcome of a test is
\star
if
x
does not pass the test, namely it gets stuck during the execution of
w
; the outcome is
\bullet
if
x
passes the test, namely it has entirely executed
w
; since the system is nondeterministic different runs of the same test on the same state can lead to different outcomes. If the outcome is always
\bullet
, then
\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{c^{\sharp}}(w)=\{\bullet\}
; if it is always
\star
, then
\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{c^{\sharp}}(w)=\{\star\}
; if instead the outcome is sometimes
\bullet
and sometimes
\star
, then
\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{c^{\sharp}}(w)=\{\bullet,\star\}
. Figure
3
illustrates an LTS and the obtained semantics. Observe that
[\![\eta(x^{\prime})]\!](a)=[\![\eta(x)]\!](a)=\{\bullet\}
since both
x
and
x^{\prime}
always pass the test
a
, while
[\![\eta(x^{\prime})]\!](b)=[\![\eta(x)]\!](b)=\{\star\}
since both
x
and
x^{\prime}
always fail the test
b
. The behaviours of
x^{\prime}
and
x
differ on the test
ab
:
x^{\prime}
always passes
ab
, while
x
may fail it. Indeed, by reading
a
,
x
can go to
z
and then get stuck when reading
b
.

We denote the induced equivalence by
\equiv^{LTS}_{*}
, and we call it may-must trace equivalence for LTS. Such a name is justified below by introducing may trace and must trace semantics, and in Remark
29
.

Recall the presentation of the monad
\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)
from Section
3.4
and its two possible quotients by the axioms (B) and (T). Let us denote the corresponding quotient maps by
q^{B}
and
q^{T}
, respectively. By applying Definition
6.4
to
(q^{B}_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}\bar{t}
and
(q^{T}_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}\bar{t}
one obtains two different semantics which we call may trace equivalence, denoted by
\equiv^{LTS}_{B}
, and must trace equivalence, denoted by
\equiv^{LTS}_{T}
. To better illustrate both of them, we focus on their algebras of observations.

By quotienting the pointed semilattice (
21
) by (B) and (T), one obtains, respectively, the semilattice with bottom and the semilattice with top freely generated by
1
, depicted on the left and on the right below.

\textstyle{\{\bullet\}=\{\bullet,\star\}}
\textstyle{\{\star\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\{\star\}=\{\bullet,\star\}}
\textstyle{\{\bullet\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

In the semilattice on the left, the top element is assigned when a state
x
may pass a test
w
, while the bottom element when
x
always fails. In the semilattice on the right, the top element is assigned when
x
can fail, while the bottom element is assigned when
x
always passes the test.
Figure
4
illustrates an example of the may semantics and the must semantics.

We conclude by observing that, since the quotient of
\mathcal{P}_{ne}(\cdot+1)
by (B) is exactly
\mathcal{P}
and, since
q^{B}\mathrel{\circ}\iota=id
, then
\equiv^{LTS}_{B}
is the standard trace semantics for LTS.

May trace (left) and must trace (right) semantics for the LTS in Figure

Remark 29
.

The testing scenario described in Example
6.4
, using the three free algebras generated by the singleton set
1=\{\bullet\}
as possible outcomes of tests, is exactly the one of the standard theory of testing equivalences
[
DH84
]
, when taking as class of tests the set of finite traces.
Hence, in this testing scenario the equivalences
\equiv^{LTS}_{*}
,
\equiv^{LTS}_{B}
, and
\equiv^{LTS}_{T}
are respectively the equivalences known as may-must (or test) testing equivalence, may testing equivalence, and must testing equivalence.

In Section
7
, we will introduce may-must, may and must semantics for systems with probability and nondeterminism, by basically applying the recipe described in this section to the three monads introduced in Section
5
. It is worth to announce here that the obtained semantics will be “backward compatible” —in a sense that will be clarified later— with
\equiv^{LTS}_{*}
,
\equiv^{LTS}_{B}
and
\equiv^{LTS}_{T}
. To prove this property we will need the following corollary of Theorem
26
.

Corollary 30
.

Let
(M,\eta,\mu)
be a submonad of
(\hat{M},\hat{\eta},\hat{\mu})
via an injective monad map
\sigma\colon M\Rightarrow\hat{M}
. Let
t\colon X\to(MX)^{A}
be a system with
M
-effects and let
\hat{t}
be the system with
\hat{M}
-effects
\sigma_{X}^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t\colon X\to(\hat{M}X)^{A}
. Let
\equiv,\hat{\equiv}\subseteq X\times X
be the trace equivalences for
t
and
\hat{t}
, respectively.
Then
\equiv\,=\,\hat{\equiv}
. ∎

Proof 6.3
.

We fix

o=\big(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M1\big)
and
\hat{o}=\big(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\longrightarrow}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\eta}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\hat{M}1\big)
.

We first transform the automaton
c=\langle o,t\rangle
to
\hat{c}=\langle\hat{o},t\rangle
and apply Theorem
26
.2 and then transform
\hat{c}=\langle\hat{o},t\rangle
to
\hat{\hat{c}}=\langle\hat{o},\hat{t}\,\rangle
and apply Theorem
26
.1.

For the determinisation, take for
a
in Theorem
26
.2 the free algebra
\mu_{1}\colon MM1\to M1
and as
\hat{a}
the
M
-algebra
(\hat{\mu}\mathrel{\circ}\sigma_{\hat{M}1})\colon M\hat{M}1\to\hat{M}1
. It is easy to see that
\hat{a}
is indeed an
M
-algebra using that
\sigma
is a monad map, its naturality, and the associativity of
\hat{\mu}
. Since
\sigma
is a monad map, the following diagram commutes showing that
\sigma_{1}
is an
M
-algebra homomorphism.

\textstyle{MM1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{M\sigma_{1}}
\textstyle{M\hat{M}1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\hat{M}1}}
\textstyle{\hat{M}\hat{M}1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\mu}}
\textstyle{M1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\textstyle{\hat{M}1}

Observe that
\hat{o}=\sigma_{1}\mathrel{\circ}o
, again since
\sigma
is a monad map. Then, by Theorem
26
.2
\hat{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}=\sigma_{1}^{A^{*}}\mathrel{\circ}\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket

where

\hat{[\![\cdot]\!]}
is the semantics obtained by determinisation of
\hat{c}
and
[\![\cdot]\!]
the one after determinisation of
c
. Since
\sigma_{1}
is injective, also
\sigma_{1}^{A^{*}}
is injective and we have that for all
x,y\in X
,
\hat{[\![\eta(x)]\!]}=\hat{[\![\eta(y)]\!]}
iff
[\![\eta(x)]\!]=[\![\eta(y)]\!]
, i.e., the semantics remains the same.

For the second step, take
\hat{\hat{a}}=\hat{\mu}\colon\hat{M}\hat{M}1\to\hat{M}1
for the determinisation of
\hat{\hat{c}}
. By definition
\hat{a}=\hat{\hat{a}}\mathrel{\circ}\sigma_{\hat{O}}
. Therefore Theorem
26
.1 guarantees that the language semantics of
\hat{\hat{c}}
(i.e., trace semantics of
\hat{t}
) again remains the same as the language semantics of
\hat{c}
(i.e, trace semantics of
t
).

7.
May / Must Traces for NPLTS

In this section, we put all the pieces together and give, using the general recipe from Section
6.4
, trace semantics for systems featuring nondeterminism, probability, and termination. We work with the monad
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}=C(\cdot+1)
and consider its two quotients
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
and
T_{\mathcal{CST}}
that we have illustrated in Section
5
. Each of these choices gives us a meaningful trace equivalence.

We start by recalling three types of coalgebras.

NPLTS
Nondeterministic probabilistic labelled transition systems, NPLTS, also known as simple Segala systems, are coalgebras for the functor
F=(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{D}(\cdot))^{A}
. Behavioural equivalence coincides with strong probabilistic bisimilarity
[
BSV04
,
Sok11
]
.

Convex NPLTS
Convex NPLTS are coalgebras for
(C+1)^{A}
. Behavioural equivalence coincides with convex probabilistic bisimilarity
[
Mio14
]
.

NPA
Nondeterministic Probabilistic automata, NPA, with observations in
O
are (for us in this paper) coalgebras for
F=O\times(C(\cdot+1))^{A}
. We explained in Section
6.4
above how to move from (convex) NPLTS to NPA, which involves two steps: (1) Adding observations and (2) Dealing with termination.

In order to define trace semantics for NPLTS via the general recipe from Section
6.4
, we first need to transform them into convex NPLTS which are systems with
C+1
-effects. Then, following Section
6.4
, we transform them into systems with
C(\cdot+1)
-effects and, finally, we add observations, so to obtain automata with
C(\cdot+1)
-effects, namely NPA.

We exploit the natural transformations
\convex
from (
6
) and
\iota
from (
1
). Given an NPLTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
we fix

\textstyle{\bar{t}=\big(X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\convex_{X}+1)^{A}}
\textstyle{(CX+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{A}}
\textstyle{(C(X+1))^{A}\big)}

(22)

and add observations as prescribed in (
19
)

\bar{o}=\big(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt}}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}}}{{\xrightarrow{\hskip 10.0pt}}}C(1+1)\big)

(23)

Now recall from Proposition
20
that the pointed convex semilattice freely generated by
1
is
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
and thus

C(1+1)=\mathcal{I}
: We easily derive that

\bar{o}(x)=\eta_{1}(!(x))=\eta_{1}(\bullet)=\{\delta_{\bullet}\}=[1,1]

for all
x\in X
, as
\delta_{\bullet}=1
(see proof of Proposition
20
).

Let
\langle\bar{o}^{\sharp},\bar{t}^{\sharp}\rangle\colon C(X+1)\to\mathcal{I}\times C(X+1)^{A}
be the determinisation of
\langle o,t\rangle
w.r.t. such algebra and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket\colon C(X+1)\to\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}
be the final coalgebra map.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 184.06448pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-34.02843pt\raise-33.47308pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\times C(X+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.53607pt\raise-40.56532pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.34224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{id_{\mathcal{I}}\times[\![\cdot]\!]^{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 159.96028pt\raise-33.47308pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 78.99435pt\raise-33.47308pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 129.96028pt\raise-33.47308pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 159.96028pt\raise-33.47308pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\times(\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}})^{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\begin{array}[]{lcl}\\
[\![S]\!](\varepsilon)&=&\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S)\\
[\![S]\!](aw)&=&[\![\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S)(a)]\!](w)\end{array}\end{array}

{defi}

We say that two states
x,y\in X
are
may-must trace equivalent
, written
x\equiv y
, if and only if
[\![\eta(x)]\!]=[\![\eta(y)]\!]
.

By instantiating (
14
) and using the presentation of the monad
C(\cdot+1)
, we can obtain a convenient inductive definition of
\langle\bar{o}^{\sharp},\bar{t}^{\sharp}\rangle
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S)&=&\begin{cases}[1,1]&\text{ if }S=x\text{;}\\
[0,0]&\text{ if }S=\star\text{;}\\
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})\minmax\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\oplus S_{2}\text{;}\\
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})+_{p}\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}\text{.}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S)(a)&=&\begin{cases}\bar{t}(x)(a)&\text{ if }S=x\text{;}\\
\star&\text{ if }S=\star\text{;}\\
\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S_{1})(a)\oplus\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S_{2})(a)&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\oplus S_{2}\text{;}\\
\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S_{1})(a)+_{p}\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S_{2})(a)&\text{ if }S=S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}\text{.}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

May trace equivalence and must trace equivalence

Now one may want to treat termination in a different way and exploit the monads
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
and
T_{\mathcal{CST}}
discussed in Section
5
. Given the monad morphisms
q^{{B}}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
and
q^{{T}}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CST}}
quotienting
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
by
(B)
and
(T)
, respectively, one can construct the transition functions

\bar{t}_{B}=(q^{{B}}_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}\bar{t}\colon X\to(T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X)^{A}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bar{t}_{T}=(q^{{T}}_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}\bar{t}\colon X\to(T_{\mathcal{CST}}X)^{A}.

(24)

For the observations, we always use the general recipe of Section
6.4
and take the observation functions:

\bar{o}_{{B}}=\big(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt}}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}}}{{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt}}}T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1\big)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bar{o}_{{T}}=\big(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt}}}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}}}{{\xrightarrow{\hskip 5.0pt}}}T_{\mathcal{CST}}1\big).

Recall from Proposition
21
and Proposition
22
that
\mathbb{M}\text{ax}_{B}=([0,1],\max,+_{p},0)
and
\mathbb{M}\text{in}_{T}=([0,1],\min,+_{p},0)
are, the free convex semilattice with bottom and, respectively, with top, generated by the singleton set
1
.
Therefore these algebraic structures gives us the determinisation of
\bar{o}_{{B}}
and
\bar{o}_{{T}}
. Since
\bar{o}_{{B}}(x)=1
and
\bar{o}_{{T}}(x)=1
for all
x\in X
, the determinisation
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}\colon T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X\to[0,1]
and
\bar{o}_{{T}}^{\sharp}\colon T_{\mathcal{CST}}X\to[0,1]
enjoy the following inductive definition.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S)&=&\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }S=x\text{;}\\
0&\text{ if }S=\star\text{;}\\
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})\max\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\oplus S_{2}\text{;}\\
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})+_{p}\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}\text{.}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{o}_{{T}}^{\sharp}(S)&=&\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }S=x\text{;}\\
0&\text{ if }S=\star\text{;}\\
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})\min\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}\oplus S_{2}\text{;}\\
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})+_{p}\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})&\text{ if }S=S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}\text{.}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

The transition functions
\bar{t}_{B}^{\sharp}\colon T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X\to(T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X)^{A}
and
\bar{t}_{T}^{\sharp}\colon T_{\mathcal{CST}}X\to(T_{\mathcal{CST}}X)^{A}
are defined in the same way like
\bar{t}^{\sharp}
above.

The coalgebras
\langle\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp},\bar{t}_{B}^{\sharp}\rangle
and
\langle\bar{o}_{{T}}^{\sharp},\bar{t}_{T}^{\sharp}\rangle
give rise to morphisms
[\![\cdot]\!]_{{B}}\colon T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X\to[0,1]^{A^{*}}
and
[\![\cdot]\!]_{{T}}\colon T_{\mathcal{CST}}X\to[0,1]^{A^{*}}
and corresponding behavioural equivalences.

{defi}

The
may trace equivalence
for the NPLTS is denoted by

\equiv_{{B}}
and defined as

x\equiv_{{B}}y\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad[\![\eta(x)]\!]_{{B}}=[\![\eta(y)]\!]_{{B}}

where
\eta
is the unit of the monad
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
.
The
must trace equivalence
for the NPLTS is denoted by
\equiv_{{T}}
and defined by

x\equiv_{{T}}y\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad[\![\eta(x)]\!]_{{T}}=[\![\eta(y)]\!]_{{T}}

for
\eta
denoting now the unit of the monad
T_{\mathcal{CST}}
.

Remark 31
.

One might exploit the generalised determinisation in different ways, but these always lead to the above semantics.

Consider the coalgebra
\langle\bar{o}_{{B}},\bar{t}\rangle\colon X\to T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1\times(T_{\mathcal{PCS}}X)^{A}
and observe that the algebra
\maxalg_{B}=([0,1],\max,+_{p},0)
, namely
\mu_{1}\colon T_{\mathcal{CSB}}T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1\to T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1
, is also a pointed convex semilattice—formally this is
\mu_{1}\mathrel{\circ}q^{{B}}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1\to T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1
. One could thus perform the generalised determinisation w.r.t. this algebra and the monad
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
and obtain an equivalence that we denote by
\equiv_{{B}}^{\prime}
.
Theorem
26
.1 guarantees however that
{\equiv_{{B}}^{\prime}}={\equiv_{{B}}}
. Similarly, one could start with the coalgebra
\langle\bar{o}_{{T}},\bar{t}\rangle
, apply the same construction and end up with an equivalence which, by Theorem
26
.1, coincides with
\equiv_{{T}}
.

Remark 32
.

By recalling that the monad
C(\cdot+1)
is presented by the algebraic theory
\mathcal{PCS}=(\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T},E_{NP})
, one can use Corollary
27
to determinise w.r.t. the term monad
T_{\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T}}
without changing the resulting semantics. The states of the systems determinised in this way are now syntactic terms, i.e, without the axioms in
E_{NP}
, and the determinisation can be expressed by means of GSOS rules. Indeed, by instantiating the GSOS rules (
17
) and (
18
) for an arbitrary signature to the signature
\Sigma_{NP}\cup\Sigma_{T}
, one obtains exactly the rules displayed in Table
1
.

The rule on the right in (
17
) gives rise to the rules for
\star
,
\oplus
and
+_{p}
in Table
1
.(a). The rule on the left gives rise to the rules for
\star
,
\oplus
and
+_{p}
in Table
1
.(b), (c) and (d) when taking as algebras of observations
\maxalg_{B}
,
\minalg_{T}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
, respectively.

The rules in (
18
) require more explanation. In the rule on the left,
o
should be instatiated with
\bar{o}_{{B}}
,
\bar{o}_{{T}}
and
\bar{o}
. By recalling that for all
x\in X
,
\bar{o}_{{B}}(x)=\bar{o}_{{T}}(x)=1
and
\bar{o}(x)=[1,1]
one obtains the rules for
x
in Table
1
.(b), (c) and (d). In order to see that the rule on the right of (
18
) gives rise to the rule for
x
in Table
1
.(a), one should take
t^{\prime}
as
(\termfun_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t
and observe that
(q^{E_{NP}}_{X})^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t^{\prime}
coincides with
\bar{t}
.

{exa}

Consider the convex closure of the NPLTS from Figure
1
.
Following Remark
32
, we can syntactically describe the convex sets of subdistributions reached by a state when performing a transition as follows:

x\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}x_{1}\oplus(x_{3}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2})

y\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}y_{1}\oplus(y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{2})\oplus((y_{2}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{3})

x_{1}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}x+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3}\qquad y_{1}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}y+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4}

x_{2}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}x_{3}\qquad x_{2}\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}x\qquad y_{2}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}y_{4}\qquad y_{3}\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}y

In the determinised system, we have

x\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}S_{2}\qquad y\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}S^{\prime}_{1}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}S^{\prime}_{2}

For

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}\oplus(x_{3}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2})

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3})\oplus(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3})

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{1}\oplus(y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{2})\oplus((y_{2}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{3})

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(y+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus((y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)

Consider now the observations associated to the terms in the may-must semantics. We have

\bar{o}^{\sharp}(x)=[1,1]=\bar{o}^{\sharp}(y)

and hence

\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{1})=[1,1]\minmax([1,1]+_{\frac{1}{2}}[1,1])=[1,1].

Analogously,
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{1})=[1,1]
.
Furtheron

\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})=([1,1]+_{\frac{1}{2}}[1,1])\minmax([0,0]+_{\frac{1}{2}}[1,1])=[\frac{1}{2},1]

and in the same way we derive
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{2})=[\frac{1}{4},1]
.

Hence,
x
and
y
are not may-must trace equivalent:

[\![x]\!](ab)=\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S_{2})\neq\bar{o}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{2})=[\![y]\!](ab)
.

However, using
\maxalg_{B}
, we get
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S_{2})=\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{2})
as the intervals obtained via the may-must observation over
S_{2},S^{\prime}_{2}
have the same upper bound
1
, which is the value returned by both
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S_{2})
and
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{2})
.
Hence,

[\![x]\!]_{B}(ab)=\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S_{2})=\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{2})=[\![y]\!]_{B}(ab)
.
More generally, it holds that
x
and
y
are may trace equivalent. We can elegantly prove this by using up-to techniques, as shown in Section
7.2
.

7.1.
Properties of the semantics

The three notions of trace equivalence for NPLTS that we have introduced above,
\equiv
,
\equiv_{{B}}
and
\equiv_{{T}}
, enjoy several desirable properties that, on the one hand, confirm the appropriateness of our semantics and, on the other, provide useful techniques for reasoning about them. In this section we show such properties while in Section
7.2
, we illustrate such techniques at work.

7.1.1.
Bisimilarity implies trace equivalence

For NPLTS, there exist two main notions of bisimilarity, which are usually called
(strong) probabilistic bisimilarity
and
convex probabilistic bisimilarity

[
SL94
]
. Originally, these relations were called (strong) bisimulation and (strong) probabilistic bisimulation, respectively. We show here that both imply may-must, may, and must trace equivalence. For this purpose, we will repeatedly use the following result from the general theory of coalgebra.

{lemC}

[
[
Rut00
, Theorem 15.1]
]
Let
F
and
G
be two endofunctors on
\mathbf{Sets}
and
c\colon X\to FX
be an
F
-coalgebra.
If there exists a natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G
, then behavioural equivalence for
c
implies behavioural equivalence for
\alpha\mathrel{\circ}c
. ∎

Theorem 33
.

Probabilistic bisimilarity (called bisimilarity in the original paper) and convex probabilistic bisimilarity (called probabilistic bisimilarity in the original paper) from
[
SL94
]
imply
\equiv
,
\equiv_{{B}}
, and
\equiv_{{T}}
.

Proof 7.1
.

Given an NPLTS, namely a coalgebra
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
, we know from
[
BSV04
,
Sok11
]
that probabilistic bisimilarity of
[
SL94
]
(denoted
\approx^{p}
) coincides with behavioural equivalence. Moreover, by
[
Mio14
]
, behavioural equivalence for
(\convex_{X}+1)^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t
coincides with convex probabilistic bisimilarity (
\approx^{c}
). Using Lemma
7.1.1
twice, we have that
\approx^{p}\,\,\subseteq\,\,\approx^{c}\,\,\subseteq\,\,\approx^{\iota}
where
\approx^{\iota}
is behavioural equivalence for
\iota_{X}^{A}\mathrel{\circ}(\convex_{X}+1)^{A}\mathrel{\circ}t
, namely
\bar{t}
from (
22
).

Now, by postcomposing
\bar{t}
with the natural transformation
C(\cdot+1)^{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\Rightarrow}}1\times C(\cdot+1)^{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{1}\times id}}{{\Rightarrow}}C(1+1)\times C(\cdot+1)^{A}
instantiated at
X
, one obtains exactly
c=\langle\bar{o},\bar{t}\rangle
. Again by Lemma
7.1.1
,
\approx^{\iota}\,\,\subseteq\,\,\approx
where
\approx
denotes here behavioural equivalence for
c
. By Theorem
6.2
.2, we now can conclude that
\approx\,\,\subseteq\,\,\equiv
. A similar argument applies for
\equiv_{{B}}
and
\equiv_{{T}}
.

7.1.2.
Backward compatibility

Both LTS and RPLTS can be regarded as special cases of NPLTS: LTS are NPLTS where all distributions are Dirac distributions; RPLTS are NPLTS where all subsets are at most singletons. Formally, we can express this using the natural transformations
\mathcal{P}_{ne}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathcal{P}_{ne}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}
and
\eta^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathcal{D}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}
.
Given an LTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}X)^{A}
we call

\tilde{t}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.53471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.91351pt\raise 5.15279pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15279pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 61.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathcal{P}_{ne}X+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.92676pt\raise 7.09224pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.34224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{P}_{ne}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{X}+1)^{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 178.16103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 148.16103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 178.16103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

the corresponding NPLTS. Similarly, the NPLTS corresponding to an RPLTS
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}
is

\tilde{t}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.53471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise The following two theorems state that
\equiv_{{B}}
,
\equiv_{{T}}
, and
\equiv
generalize—through these translations—the trace equivalences for RPLTS and LTS introduced in Example
6.4
and Example
6.4
.

Theorem 34

(Backward Compatibility for RPLTSs)
.

Let
\equiv^{RP}
be trace equivalence defined on an RPLTS
t
and
\equiv_{{B}}
,
\equiv_{{T}}
and
\equiv
the may, must and may-must equivalences defined on the corresponding NPLTS
\tilde{t}
. Then

\equiv^{RP}\,\,=\,\,\equiv\,\,=\,\,\equiv_{{B}}\,\,=\,\,\equiv_{{T}}

Proof 7.2
.

We prove
\equiv^{RP}\,\,=\,\,\equiv
by applying Corollary
30
to the injective monad map
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}{(\cdot+1)}
from Lemma
23
. In order to do so, it is enough to observe that the following diagram commutes: the two triangles by definition of
\tilde{t}
and
\chi^{\mathcal{D}}
from Lemma
6
; the square by Lemma
3
.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{t}}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\eta^{\mathcal{P}_{ne}}{\mathcal{D}}_{X}+1)^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{(\chi^{\mathcal{D}}_{X}+1)^{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(X+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\chi^{\mathcal{D}}(\cdot+1)^{A}_{X}}}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\convex_{X}+1)^{A}}
\textstyle{(CX+1)^{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{A}}
\textstyle{C(X+1)^{A}}

In the diagram above, the topmost arrow is exactly

used to define

over

(Example

). The down-and-right arrow

To prove

on

).

The proof for

is obtained by replacing in the previous paragraph
B
by

.

Theorem 35

(Backward Compatibility for LTSs)
.

,

. Then

.

, but replacing

by

. The proof for

7.1.3.
The bialgebra of probabilistic traces

The generalised determinisation outlined in Section
6.2
allows to think of the final coalgebra as a denotational universe of behaviours and of the final map
[\![\cdot]\!]
as a denotational semantics assigning to each state its behaviour. We better illustrate this idea here by considering the may-must semantics, but the same arguments hold for the may and the must cases.

The set
\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}
of nondeterministic-probabilistic languages, namely functions
\varphi\colon A^{*}\to\mathcal{I}
, carries at the same time the final coalgebra
\langle\epsilon,\dder\rangle\colon\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}\to\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}
(defined as in Section
6.2
for
O=\mathcal{I}
) and a pointed convex semilattice.
The latter is defined as the pointwise extension of the algebra of observations
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
, see Proposition
20
. This means that the structure of pointed convex semilattice is defined as follows for all
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}
and for all
w\in A^{*}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(\varphi_{1}\oplus^{\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}}\varphi_{2})(w)&=&\varphi_{1}(w)\minmax\varphi_{2}(w)\\
(\varphi_{1}+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}}\varphi_{2})(w)&=&\varphi_{1}(w)+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}}\varphi_{2}(w)\\
\star^{\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}}(w)&=&[0,0]\end{array}

By the generalised determinisation, we know that the final coalgebra map
[\![\cdot]\!]\colon C(X+1)\to\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}
is also a homomorphism of pointed convex semilattices which means that

[\![S_{1}\oplus S_{2}]\!]=[\![S_{1}]\!]\oplus^{\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}}[\![S_{2}]\!]\text{, }[\![S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}]\!]=[\![S_{1}]\!]+_{p}^{\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}}[\![S_{2}]\!]\text{ and }[\![\star]\!]=\star^{\mathcal{I}^{A^{*}}}

for all
S_{1},S_{2}\in C(X+1)
. This guarantees the following result.

Theorem 36
.

Let
\cong
,
\cong_{B}
and
\cong_{T}
be the kernels of
[\![\cdot]\!]
,
[\![\cdot]\!]_{{B}}
, and
[\![\cdot]\!]_{{T}}
, respectively. Then
\cong
,
\cong_{B}
, and
\cong_{T}
are congruences w.r.t.
\oplus
and
+_{p}
.

The theorem above is an instantiation of Theorem
6.2
.1 to our construction for NPLTS. By instantiating Theorem
6.2
.3, we have the following useful fact.

Theorem 37
.

Up-to context is compatible for each of the three equivalences.∎

In the next section, we illustrate the implication of such result. Since
x\equiv y
iff
\eta(x)\cong\eta(y)
, hereafter we will sometimes write
\equiv
instead of
\cong
. Similarly for
\equiv_{{B}}
and
\equiv_{{T}}
.

7.2.
Coinduction Up-to

As anticipated in Theorem
37
,
\equiv
,
\equiv_{{B}}
, and
\equiv_{{T}}
can be proved coinductively by means of bisimulation up-to. In order to define uniformly the proof techniques for the three equivalences, we let
\equiv_{i}
to range over
\equiv
,
\equiv_{{B}}
, and
\equiv_{{T}}
;
T_{i}
to range over
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
,
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
, and
T_{\mathcal{CST}}
;
\bar{t}^{\sharp}_{i}
, over
\bar{t}^{\sharp}
,
\bar{t}_{B}^{\sharp}
, and
\bar{t}_{T}^{\sharp}
;
\bar{o}^{\sharp}_{i}
over
\bar{o}^{\sharp}
,
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}
, and
\bar{o}_{{T}}^{\sharp}
.

{defi}

Let
(X,t)
be an NPLTS and
(T_{i}X,\langle\bar{o}^{\sharp}_{i},\bar{t}^{\sharp}_{i}\rangle)
the corresponding determinised system. A relation
\mathcal{R}\subseteq T_{i}X\times T_{i}X
is a bisimulation iff for all
(S_{1},S_{2})\in R
it holds that

(1)

\bar{o}^{\sharp}_{i}(S_{1})=\bar{o}^{\sharp}_{i}(S_{2})
and

(2)

\bar{t}^{\sharp}_{i}(S_{1})(a)\,\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}\,\bar{t}^{\sharp}_{i}(S_{2})(a)
for all
a\in A
.

The coinduction proof principle (see e.g.
[
BPPR17
]
) asserts that for all
x,y\in X
,
x\equiv_{i}y
iff there exists a bisimulation
\mathcal{R}
such that
x\,\mathcal{R}\,y
.

We can now prove that states
x,y
in Figure
1
are may trace equivalent by showing that there exists a bisimulation
\mathcal{R}
such that
x\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}y
on the system determinized using as algebra of observations
\maxalg_{B}
.

{exa}

Consider the NPLTS
(X,t)
depicted in Figure
1
, and discussed in Example
Figure
5
shows the determinization of the system, where
the terms are, as already partially mentioned in Example
as follows

S_{1}=x_{1}\oplus(x_{3}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2})\qquad S_{2}=(x+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3})\oplus(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3})

S^{\prime}_{1}=y_{1}\oplus(y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{2})\oplus((y_{2}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{3})

S^{\prime}_{2}=(y+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus((y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)

S_{3}=\star\oplus(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}x)\qquad S^{\prime}_{3}=\star\oplus(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y)

and the depicted transitions are those given by
\bar{t}^{\sharp}
.

Figure 5.
Determinization

The determinized NPLTS is a system with infinitely many states, which are given by the presence of cycles in the original system. In the determinization of the automaton with algebra of observation
\maxalg_{B}
, each state
S
is assigned an observation
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S)\in[0,1]
.
We prove that
x
and
y
are may trace equivalent by exhibiting the following bisimulation
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=

\displaystyle\{(x,y),(S_{1},S^{\prime}_{1}),(S_{2},S^{\prime}_{2}),(S_{3},S^{\prime}_{3})\}

\displaystyle\cup\{((S_{i}+_{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\star)\oplus\star,(S^{\prime}_{i}+_{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\star)\oplus\star)|\,1\leq i\leq 3,n\geq 1\}

The relation satisfies the two clauses required by Definition
7.2
of bisimulation. As it emerges from Figure
5
, the clause on transitions (clause 2) is satisfied by each pair in the relation.
As to the clause on the observation (clause 1), we can derive as in Example
7
that for every pair
(S,S^{\prime})\in\{(x,y),(S_{1},S^{\prime}_{1}),(S_{2},S^{\prime}_{2}),(S_{3},S^{\prime}_{3})\}
it holds

\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S)=\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime})
.

Finally, clause 1 also holds for the remaining pairs, since for
1\leq i\leq 3
and
n\geq 1
we have

\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}((S_{i}+_{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\star)\oplus\star)=(\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S_{i})+_{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}0)\max 0=(\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{i})+_{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}0)\max 0=\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}((S^{\prime}_{i}+_{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\star)\oplus\star)

Hence,
\mathcal{R}
is a bisimulation.

As shown in Example
7
,
x,y
are not bisimilar if the algebra of observations for the must equivalence, i.e,
\minalg_{T}
, is used instead of the one for the may equivalence, since
\bar{o}_{{T}}^{\sharp}(S_{2})\neq\bar{o}_{{T}}^{\sharp}(S^{\prime}_{2})
. Analogously, they are not equivalent if we take the may-must algebra of observation
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{I},[0,0]}
.

To make this proof principle more effective, one can use
up-to techniques

[
Mil89
,
PS12
]
. Particularly relevant for us is up-to
contextual closure
which, for all relations
\mathcal{R}\subseteq T_{i}(X)\times T_{i}(X)
, is defined inductively by the following rules.

\frac{S\mathrel{\mathcal{R}}S^{\prime}}{S\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S^{\prime}}\quad\quad\frac{-}{*\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}*}

\frac{S_{1}\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S_{1}^{\prime}S_{2}\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S_{2}^{\prime}}{S_{1}\oplus S_{2}\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S_{1}^{\prime}\oplus S_{2}^{\prime}}

\frac{S_{1}\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S_{1}^{\prime}S_{2}\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S_{2}^{\prime}}{S_{1}+_{p}S_{2}\mathrel{\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})}S_{1}^{\prime}+_{p}S_{2}^{\prime}}

{defi}

Bisimulations up-to context are defined as in Definition
7.2
, but with
\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})
instead of
\mathcal{R}
in point (2).

By virtue of the general theory in
[
BPPR17
]
, one has that
\mathit{Ctx}
is a
sound
up-to technique, that is
x\equiv_{i}y
iff there exists a bisimulation up-to context
\mathcal{R}
such that
x\,\mathcal{R}\,y
.
Actually, the theory in
[
BPPR17
]
guarantees a stronger property known as compatibility
[
San98
,
Pou07
,
PS12
]
. Intuitively, this means that the technique is sound and it can be safely combined with other compatible up-to techniques. We refer the interested reader to
[
PS12
]
for a detailed introduction to compatible up-to techniques.

We conclude with an example illustrating a finite bisimulation up-to context witnessing that the states
x
and
y
from Figure
1
are in
\equiv_{{B}}
.

{exa}

Consider the NPLTS depicted in Figure
1
.
We have seen in Example
7.2
how to prove that
x\equiv_{{B}}y
by exhibiting a bisimulation on
(T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X,\langle\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp},\bar{t}_{B}^{\sharp}\rangle)
relating them. However, due to the presence of cycles, the determinization of the NPLTS is infinite and the bisimulation relation contains infinitely many pairs.

With bisimulations up-to, only few pairs are necessary.
Indeed, we prove that the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\{

\displaystyle(x,y),(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{3},y_{4}),

\displaystyle(x_{3}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2},(y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{2})\oplus((y_{2}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{3}))\}

is a bisimulation up-to context.
First, note that the observation is trivially the same for all pairs in the relation, since
\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp}(S)=1
for all
S
in the relation.
heck that the clauses of bisimulation up-to context on the transitions are satisfied. Consider the first pair.
In
(T_{\mathcal{CSB}}X,\langle\bar{o}_{{B}}^{\sharp},\bar{t}_{B}^{\sharp}\rangle)
, we have

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}x_{1}\oplus(x_{3}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2})

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}y_{1}\oplus(y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{2})\oplus((y_{2}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{3})

The reached states are in
\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})
by the second and fourth pairs of
\mathcal{R}
.
For any action
a^{\prime}\neq a
, we have

x\overset{a^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}\star
,
y\overset{a^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}\star

and
\star\,\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})\,\star
.

The second and the third pairs can be checked in a similar way.
For the fourth pair, we have

x_{3}+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{2}\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3}

(y_{4}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{2})\oplus((y_{2}+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{3})\overset{b}{\longrightarrow}(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus((\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)

We observe that

\displaystyle(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus((\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B)}}{{=}}(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus((\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})+_{\frac{1}{2}}\star)\oplus\star

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(C)}}{{=}}(\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4})\oplus\star

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(B)}}{{=}}\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4}

and we conclude by
\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}x_{3}\,\mathit{Ctx}(\mathcal{R})\,\star+_{\frac{1}{2}}y_{4}
.
The cases for
a
and
c
are simpler.

8.
From the global to the local perspective

Usually trace semantics for NPLTS is defined in terms of
schedulers
, or
resolutions
: intuitively, a scheduler resolves the nondeterminism by choosing, at each step of the execution of an NPLTS, one of its possible transitions; the transition systems resulting from these choices are called resolutions.

This perspective on trace semantics is somehow opposed to ours, where the generalised determinisation keeps track of all possible executions at once. In this sense, the determinisation provides a perspective which is
global
, opposite to those of resolutions that are
local
. In this section, we show that our semantics can be characterised through such local views, by means of resolutions, defined as follows.

{defi}

Let
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
be an NPLTS. A
(randomized) resolution
for
t
is a triple
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
where
Y
is a set of states,
\corr\colon Y\to X
is
the correspondence function
, and
r\colon Y\to(\mathcal{D}Y+1)^{A}
is an RPLTS such that for all
y\in Y
and
a\in A
,

(1)

r(y)(a)=\star
iff
t(\corr(y))(a)=\star
,

(2)

if
r(y)(a)\neq\star
then
\mathcal{D}(\corr)(r(y)(a))\in\convex(t(\corr(y))(a))
.

Intuitively, this means that a resolution of an NPLTS is built from the original system by discarding internal nondeterminism (the possibility to perform multiple transitions labelled with the same action) and in such a way that the structure of the original system is preserved.

{exa}

Consider the NPLTS on the left of Figure
1
.
The RPLTSs
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
in Figure
6
are two resolutions for it, both having the identity as correspondence function. In the resolution
\mathcal{R}_{1}
, the nondeterministic choice of
x
is resolved by choosing the leftmost
a
-transition. The resolution
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is obtained by taking a convex combination of the two distributions
\delta_{x_{1}}
and
\Delta_{2}
, assigning one half probability to each of them.
