The resolutions
\mathcal{R}_{1}
(left),
\mathcal{R}_{2}
(center), and
\mathcal{R}_{3}
(right).

The reason why we take arbitrary
\corr
functions, rather than just injective ones, is that the original NPLTS might contain cycles, in which case we want to allow the resolution to take different choices at different times.

{exa}

In order to understand how a resolution allows to resolve differently nondeterministic choices at different times, when cycles occur in the original system, consider the NPLTS on the left of Figure
1
and its resolution
\mathcal{R}_{3}
in
Figure
6
. In the latter, the state space is enlarged with state
x_{4}
, which is mapped to
x
by the correspondence function. On the remaining states, the correspondence function is the identity over
X
. In this resolution,
x
first chooses the right-hand transition of the original NPLTS, and at the next cycle iteration, represented by
x_{4}
, the left-hand transition is chosen. Observe that
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}_{3}}(x)(abab)=0
.

Given a resolution
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
, we define the function
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}\colon Y\to[0,1]^{A^{*}}
inductively for
y\in Y
and
w\in A^{*}
as

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad 1\text{;}

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }r(y)(a)=\star\text{;}\\
\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w)&\text{ if }r(y)(a)=\Delta\text{.}\\
\end{cases}

Intuitively, for all states
y\in Y
,
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)
gives the probability of
y
performing the trace
w
. For instance, in the resolutions in Figure
6
,

\pprob_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}(x)(abab)=\frac{1}{2}
and
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}(x)(abab)=\frac{3}{16}
.

Now, given an NPLTS
(X,t)
, define
\lfloor\!\lfloor\cdot\rfloor\!\rfloor\colon X\to[0,1]^{A^{*}}
by, for
x\in X
and
w\in A^{*}
,

\displaystyle\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)=\bigsqcup\{\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)\,\mid\,\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)\text{ is a resolution of }(X,t)\text{ and }\corr(y)=x\}\text{.}

Similarly, we define

\displaystyle\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w)=\bigsqcap\{\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)\,\mid\,\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)\text{ is a resolution of }(X,t)\text{ and }\corr(y)=x\}\text{.}

The following theorem states that the global view of trace semantics developed in Section
7
coincides with the trace semantics defined locally via resolutions.

Theorem 38

(Global/local correspondence)
.

Let
(X,t)
be an NPLTS. For all
x\in X
and
w\in A^{*}
, it holds that

[\![x]\!](w)=[\,\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w),\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\,]\text{.}

Corollary 39
.

Let
(X,t)
be an NPLTS. For all
x\in X
and
w\in A^{*}
,

[\![x]\!]_{{B}}(w)=\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)
and
[\![x]\!]_{{T}}(w)=\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w)
.

Theorem
38
and Corollary
39
provide a characterisation of
\equiv
,
\equiv_{{B}}
and
\equiv_{{T}}
in terms of resolutions. Their proofs are presented in Section
8.1
.
Moreover, we show in Section
8.2
that
\equiv_{{B}}
coincides with the randomized
\sqcup
-trace equivalence investigated in
[
Cas18
]
and inspired by
[
BDL14b
,
BDL14a
]
.

8.1.
Proof of the global/local correspondence theorems

Given a resolution
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
, we define the function
\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}\colon Y\to(\mathcal{D}(Y+1))^{A^{*}}
inductively as

\displaystyle\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\delta_{y}\text{;}

\displaystyle\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\begin{cases}\delta_{\star}&\text{ if }r(y)(a)=\star\text{;}\\
\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w)&\text{ if }r(y)(a)=\Delta\text{.}\\
\end{cases}

Intuitively, this assigns to each state
y\in Y
and word
w\in A^{*}
a subdistribution over
Y
, which is the state of the determinised system that
y
reaches via
w
.

Let
o^{\prime\sharp}\colon\mathcal{D}(Y+1)\to[0,1]
be the function assigning to a subdistribution
\Delta
its total mass, namely
1-\Delta(\star)
. More formally, this is defined inductively as

o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\Delta=\delta_{\star}\text{;}\\
1&\text{ if }\Delta=\delta_{y}\text{ for }y\in Y\text{;}\\
o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta_{1})+_{p}o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta_{2})&\text{ if }\Delta=\Delta_{1}+_{p}\Delta_{2}\text{.}\end{cases}

Lemma 40
.

o^{\prime\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof 8.1
.

We prove that
o^{\prime\sharp}(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w))=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)
for all
y\in Y
and
w\in A^{*}
. The proof proceeds by induction on
w
.

Base case:
w=\varepsilon
.

\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\varepsilon)=1=o^{\prime\sharp}(\delta_{y})=o^{\prime\sharp}(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\varepsilon))

Inductive case:
w=aw^{\prime}
. If
r(y)(a)=\star
, then

\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime})=0=o^{\prime\sharp}(\delta_{\star})=o^{\prime\sharp}(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime}))\text{.}

If
r(y)(a)=\Delta
, then

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime})

(definition)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot o^{\prime\sharp}(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime}))

(IH)

\displaystyle=o^{\prime\sharp}(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime})))

(
o^{\prime\sharp}
hom.)

\displaystyle=o^{\prime\sharp}(\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime}))

(definition)

Given an NPLTS
(X,t)
, we define the function
\reach\colon X\to(C(X+1))^{A^{*}}
inductively as

\displaystyle\reach(x)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\delta_{x}\}\text{;}

\displaystyle\reach(x)(aw)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\{\delta_{\star}\}&\text{ if }t(x)(a)=\star\text{;}\\
\underset{{}_{\Delta\in\convex(S)}}{\displaystyle\bigoplus}\displaystyle\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w)&\text{ if }t(x)(a)=S{.}\\
\end{cases}

For each NPLTS
(X,t)
, we have a function
\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle\colon C(X+1)\to C(X+1)^{A^{*}}
defined for all
S\in C(X+1)
and
w\in A^{*}
as

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle\!\langle S\rangle\!\rangle(\varepsilon)&=&S\text{;}\\
\langle\!\langle S\rangle\!\rangle(aw)&=&\langle\!\langle\bar{t}^{\sharp}(S)(a)\rangle\!\rangle(w)\text{.}\\
\end{array}

The following property follows directly from the inductive definitions of
[\![\cdot]\!]
and
\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle
.

Lemma 41
.

[\![\cdot]\!]=\bar{o}^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle

Lemma 42
.

\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\reach

Proof 8.2
.

The proof goes by induction on
w\in A^{*}
.

Base case: if
w=\varepsilon
, then
\reach(x)(\varepsilon)=\{\delta_{x}\}=\eta(x)=\langle\!\langle\eta(x)\rangle\!\rangle(\varepsilon)
.

Inductive case:
w=aw^{\prime}
. If
t(x)(a)=\star
, then
\langle\!\langle\eta(x)\rangle\!\rangle(aw^{\prime})=\langle\!\langle\bar{t}^{\sharp}(\{\delta_{x}\})(a)\rangle\!\rangle(w^{\prime})=\langle\!\langle\{\delta_{\star}\}\rangle\!\rangle(w^{\prime})=\{\delta_{\star}\}=\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})
.

If
t(x)(a)=S
, then
\reach(x)(aw)=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\convex(S)}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w)
. By induction hypothesis, the latter is equal to

\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\convex(S)}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(x^{\prime})\cdot\langle\!\langle\eta(x^{\prime})\rangle\!\rangle(w^{\prime})
. Since
\bar{t}^{\sharp}
is a homomorphism of convex semilattices, then
\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle
is a homomorphism of convex semilattices.
Hence, the latter is equal to

\langle\!\langle\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\convex(S)}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(x^{\prime})\cdot\eta(x^{\prime})\rangle\!\rangle(w)

that is
\langle\!\langle\convex(S)\rangle\!\rangle(w^{\prime})=\langle\!\langle\bar{t}^{\sharp}(\{\delta_{x}\})(a)\rangle\!\rangle(w^{\prime})=\langle\!\langle\eta(x)\rangle\!\rangle(aw^{\prime})
.

Proposition 43
.

[\![\cdot]\!]\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\bar{o}^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\reach

Proof 8.3
.

By Lemma
41
,
[\![\cdot]\!]\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\bar{o}^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\circ}\eta
. By Lemma
42
,
\bar{o}^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\langle\!\langle\cdot\rangle\!\rangle\mathrel{\circ}\eta=\bar{o}^{\sharp}\mathrel{\circ}\reach
.

Proposition 44
.

Let
(X,t)
be an NPLTS and let
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
be one of its resolutions. Let
x\in X
and
y\in Y
such that
\corr(y)=x
. For all
w\in A^{*}
,

\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w))\in\reach(x)(w)\text{.}

Proof 8.4
.

By induction on the structure of
w
.
If
w=\epsilon
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\delta_{y})

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}

\displaystyle\in\{\delta_{x}\}

\displaystyle=\reach(x)(\epsilon)

If
w=aw^{\prime}
and
t(x)(a)=\star
, then
r(y)(a)=\star
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\delta_{\star})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\star}

\displaystyle\in\{\delta_{\star}\}

\displaystyle=\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})

If
t(x)(a)\neq\star
then we have
r(y)(a)\neq\star
. Let
r(y)(a)=\Delta\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
. We have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime}))

By the inductive hypothesis, for each
y^{\prime}
we have

\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime}))\in\reach(\corr(y^{\prime}))(w^{\prime})\text{.}

Hence, by the definition of Minkowski sum,

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\reach(\corr(y^{\prime}))(w^{\prime})

Since
\mathcal{R}
is a resolution, there is a
\Delta^{\prime}\in\convex(t(x)(a))
such that
\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta)=\Delta^{\prime}
. The latter means that
\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\sum_{\{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)|\corr(y^{\prime})=x^{\prime}\}}\Delta(y^{\prime})
, and thus:

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\reach(\corr(y^{\prime}))(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})

as easily follows from the axioms of convex algebras. We can then conclude by the definition of
\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})

\displaystyle\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigoplus_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\convex(t(a)(x))}\;\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\reach(x)(aw^{\prime}).

Proposition 45
.

Let
(X,t)
be an NPLTS. For all
x\in X
and
w\in A^{*}
, if
\Delta\in\reach(x)(w)
then there exists a resolution
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
and a state
y\in Y
such that

(1)

\corr(y)=x
and

(2)

\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w))=\Delta
.

Proof 8.5
.

The proof proceeds by induction on
w\in A^{*}
.

In the base case
w=\varepsilon
. For all
x\in X
and
a\in A
such that
t(x)(a)\neq\star
, we can choose one distribution
\Delta_{x,a}\in t(x)(a)
. Then, we take
\mathcal{R}=(X,id_{X},r)
where
r\colon X\to(\mathcal{D}X+1)^{A}
is defined for all
x\in X
and
a\in A
as

r(x)(a)=\begin{cases}\star&\text{ if }t(x)(a)=\star\text{;}\\
\Delta_{x,a}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

By construction
\mathcal{R}
is a resolution. Then we take
x
as the selected state
y
of the resolution
\mathcal{R}
. Since the correspondence function is
id_{X}
,
(1)
is immediately satisfied. Now, by definition,
\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(x)(\varepsilon)=\delta_{x}
and
\reach(x)(\varepsilon)=\{\delta_{x}\}
. We conclude by observing that
\mathcal{D}(id_{X}+1)(\delta_{x})=\delta_{x}\in\{\delta_{x}\}=\reach(x)(\varepsilon)
.

In the inductive case
w=aw^{\prime}
. Now we have two cases to consider: either
t(x)(a)=\star
or
t(x)(a)=S
for
S\in\mathcal{P}_{ne}\mathcal{D}(X)
.

Assume
t(x)(a)=\star
. Then
\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})=\{\delta_{\star}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{R}=(X,id_{X},r)
be the resolution defined as in the base case, and take
x
as the selected state
y
of the resolution
\mathcal{R}
. Since the correspondence function is
id_{X}
,
(1)
is immediately satisfied. Since
\mathcal{R}
is a resolution,
t(x)(a)=\star
implies
r(x)(a)=\star
. Hence,
\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(x)(a)=\delta_{\star}
and
\reach(x)(a)=\{\delta_{\star}\}
. We conclude by
\mathcal{D}(id_{X}+1)(\delta_{\star})=\delta_{\star}\in\{\delta_{\star}\}=\reach(x)(a)
.

Assume
t(x)(a)=S
.
Then

\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\convex(S)}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})\text{.}

By Lemma
19
,
it holds that

\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})=\bigcup_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\convex(S)}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})\text{.}

Hence,
\Delta\in\reach(x)(aw^{\prime})
if and only if
there exists a
\Delta^{\prime}\in\convex(S)
such that

\Delta\in\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})\text{.}

This is in turn equivalent to saying (by the definition of Minkowski sum) that for every
x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})
there exists a
\Delta^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}\in\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})
such that

\Delta=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\cdot\Delta^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}\text{.}

We can now use the induction hypothesis on
\Delta^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}\in\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})
: for each
\Delta^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}\in\reach(x^{\prime})(w^{\prime})
there exists a resolution
\mathcal{R}_{x^{\prime}}=(Y_{x^{\prime}},\corr_{x^{\prime}},r_{x^{\prime}})
and a
y_{x^{\prime}}\in Y_{x^{\prime}}
such that

(c)

\corr_{x^{\prime}}(y_{x^{\prime}})=x^{\prime}
and

(d)

\mathcal{D}(\corr_{x^{\prime}}+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}_{{x^{\prime}}}}(y_{x^{\prime}})(w))=\Delta^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}
.

Now we construct the coproduct of all the resolutions
\mathcal{R}_{{x^{\prime}}}
. Take
Z
to be the disjoint union of all the
Y_{x^{\prime}}
and define
\corr_{Z}\colon Z\to X
as

\corr_{Z}(z)=\corr_{x^{\prime}}(z)
if
z\in Y_{x^{\prime}}
. Similarly, we define
r_{Z}\colon Z\to(\mathcal{D}Z+1)^{A}
as
r_{Z}(z)=r_{x^{\prime}}(z)
if
z\in Y_{x^{\prime}}
. By construction,
\mathcal{R}_{Z}=(Z,\corr_{Z},r_{Z})
is a resolution of
(X,t)
.

Let
\mathcal{R^{\prime}}=(X,id_{X},r^{\prime})
be a resolution defined as in the base case, i.e., by arbitrarily choosing a distribution
\Delta_{x,a}\in t(x)(a)
, for any
x
and
a
, as value of
r^{\prime}(x)(a)
, whenever
t(x)(a)\neq\star
.
We define the resolution
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
needed to conclude the proof as follows:
the state space is
Y=Z+X+\{y\}
, namely the disjoint union of
Z
, of
X
, and of the singleton containing a fresh state
y
; the correspondence function
\corr\colon Y\to X
and the transition function
r\colon Y\to(\mathcal{D}Y+1)^{A}
are defined for all
u\in Y
as

\corr(u)=\begin{cases}\corr_{Z}(u)&\text{ if }u\in Z\text{,}\\
id_{X}(u)&\text{ if }u\in X\text{,}\\
x&\text{ if }u=y\text{,}\end{cases}

r(u)(b)=\begin{cases}r_{Z}(u)(b)&\text{ if }u\in Z\text{,}\\
r^{\prime}(u)(b)&\text{ if }u\in X\text{,}\\
\Delta_{x,b}&\text{ if }u=y,a\neq b,t(x)(b)\neq\star\text{,}\\
\star&\text{ if }u=y,a\neq b,t(x)(b)=\star\text{,}\\
\Delta^{\prime\prime}&\text{ if }u=y,\,a=b\\
\end{cases}

where
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is the distribution having as support the set of states
\{y_{x^{\prime}}|x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)\}\subseteq Z
, and such that
\Delta^{\prime\prime}(y_{x^{\prime}})=\Delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\text{.}
Note that
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
is a distribution, since
\Delta^{\prime}
is and since we are taking exactly one
y_{x^{\prime}}
for each
x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})
.

The fact that
\mathcal{R}
is a resolution follows from
\mathcal{R}_{Z}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
being resolutions and

y
respecting — by construction — the conditions of resolution: indeed
\corr(y)=x
, and

•

if
a\neq b
and
t(x)(b)\neq\star
, then

r(y)(b)=\Delta_{x,b}
and

\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta_{x,b})=\mathcal{D}(id_{X})(\Delta_{x,b})=\Delta_{x,b}\in t(x)(b)

•

if
a\neq b
and
t(x)(b)=\star
, then
r(y)(b)=\star
;

•

if
a=b
, then
r(y)(b)=\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta^{\prime\prime})=\Delta^{\prime}
, with
\Delta^{\prime}\in\convex(t(x)(a))
.

To conclude the proof we only need to show that (1) and (2) hold. The former is trivially satisfied by definition of
\corr
. For (2), we derive:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\sum_{y_{x^{\prime}}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime\prime})}(\Delta^{\prime\prime}(y_{x^{\prime}})\cdot\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y_{x^{\prime}})(w^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}(\Delta^{\prime}({x^{\prime}})\cdot\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y_{x^{\prime}})(w^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}({x^{\prime}})\cdot(\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y_{x^{\prime}})(w^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta^{\prime})}\Delta^{\prime}({x^{\prime}})\cdot\Delta^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta

Proof 8.6

(Proof of Theorem
38
)
.

Before starting with the actual proof, we need the following elementary observation: for all
f\colon X\to Y
and
\Delta\in\mathcal{D}(X+1)
, it holds that

o^{\prime\sharp}(\mathcal{D}(f+1)(\Delta))=o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta)\text{,}

(25)

namely, the total mass is preserved by applying
\mathcal{D}(f+1)
.

Now, suppose that
[\![\eta(x)]\!](w)=[p,q]
for some
p,q\in[0,1]
with
p\leq q
.
By Proposition
43
, it holds that
\bar{o}^{\sharp}(\reach(x)(w))=[p,q]
. By definition of
\bar{o}^{\sharp}
there exists
\Delta_{\min},\Delta_{\max}\in\reach(x)(w)
such that the total mass of
\Delta_{\min}=p
, the total mass of
\Delta_{\max}=q
and for an arbitrary
\Delta\in\reach(x)
, its total mass is in between
p
and
q
. In other words,

(a)

o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta_{\min})=p
,

(b)

o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta_{\max})=q
and

(c)

p\leq o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta)\leq q
for all
\Delta\in\reach(x)
.

By Proposition
44
, for all resolutions
\mathcal{R}
, states
y
such that
\corr(y)=x
, and distributions
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)=\Delta^{\prime}
, one has that
\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\Delta^{\prime})\in\reach(x)(w)
. By (c),
p\leq o^{\prime\sharp}(\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\Delta^{\prime}))\leq q
and, by (
25
),
p\leq o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta^{\prime})\leq q
. This means
p\leq o^{\prime\sharp}(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w))\leq q
that, by Lemma
40
, coincides with
p\leq\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)\leq q
. This proves that
\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w)\geq p
and
\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\leq q
.

We now prove that
\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w)\leq p
; the proof for
\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\geq q
is analogous.

By Proposition
45
, there exist resolutions
\mathcal{R}
, a state
y
and a distribution
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that

(d)

\corr(y)=x
,

(e)

\mathcal{D}(\corr+1)(\Delta^{\prime\prime})=\Delta_{\min}
,

(f)

\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)=\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.

By (e) and (
25
), one immediately has that
o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta^{\prime\prime})=o^{\prime\sharp}(\Delta_{\min})=p
. By (f), the latter means that
o^{\prime\sharp}(\reachres_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w))=p
that, by Lemma
40
, allows to conclude that
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)=p
.
This proves that
\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w)\leq p
.

Proof 8.7

(Proof of Corollary
39
.)
.

Consider the monad morphism
q^{{B}}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
quotienting
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
by
(B)
(see Section
7
), and let
\eta^{B},\mu^{B}
respectively denote the unit and multiplication of the monad
T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
.
Following Remark
31
(by Theorem
26
.1) we have

[\![\cdot]\!]_{{B}}\circ\eta^{B}=[\![\cdot]\!]_{B^{\prime}}\circ\eta

where
[\![\cdot]\!]_{B^{\prime}}:T_{\mathcal{PCS}}X\to T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1^{A^{*}}
is the semantic map induced by the determinisation of

\langle\bar{o}_{{B}},\bar{t}\rangle\colon X\to T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1\times(T_{\mathcal{PCS}}X)^{A}
using the algebra
\mu^{B}_{1}\mathrel{\circ}q^{{B}}_{T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1\to T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1
.
The monad map
q^{{B}}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CSB}}
gives a
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
-algebra morphism
q^{{B}}_{1}\colon(T_{\mathcal{PCS}}1,\mu_{1})\to(T_{\mathcal{CSB}}1,\mu^{B}_{1}\circ q^{{B}}_{T_{\mathcal{PCS}}1})
.
By
Theorem
26
.2 we have

[\![\cdot]\!]_{B^{\prime}}={q^{{B}}_{1}}^{A^{*}}\circ[\![\cdot]\!].

Hence, we derive

[\![\cdot]\!]_{{B}}\circ\eta^{B}={q^{{B}}_{1}}^{A^{*}}\circ[\![\cdot]\!]\circ\eta.

For an interval
[p,q]
,

q^{{B}}_{1}([p,q])=q
.
Then by Theorem
38
we conclude

\displaystyle({q^{{B}}_{1}}^{A^{*}}\circ[\![\eta(x)]\!])(w)

\displaystyle=q^{{B}}_{1}([\![\eta(x)]\!](w))

\displaystyle=q^{{B}}_{1}([\,\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil(w),\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\,])

\displaystyle=\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\text{.}

To prove that
[\![\eta(\cdot)]\!]_{T}=\lceil\!\lceil\cdot\rceil\!\rceil
we proceed in the same way, but taking the monad morphism
q^{{T}}\colon T_{\mathcal{PCS}}\Rightarrow T_{\mathcal{CST}}
quotienting
T_{\mathcal{PCS}}
by
(T)
.

8.2.
Coincidence with randomized
\sqcup
-trace equivalence

Let
t\colon X\to(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{D}X)^{A}
be an NPLTS. A
fully probabilistic resolution
for
t
is a triple
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
such that
Y
is a set,
\corr\colon Y\to X
, and
r\colon Y\to(A\times\mathcal{D}Y)+1
such that for every
y\in Y
and
a\in A
:

if
r(y)=\langle a,\Delta\rangle
then
\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta)\in\convex(t(\corr(y))(a))
.

While resolutions resolve only internal nondeterminism, fully probabilistic resolutions resolve both internal and external nondeterminism. Indeed, in a resolution a state can perform transitions with different labels, while in a fully probabilistic resolution a state can perform at most one transition. Moreover, a state
y
in a fully probabilistic resolutions might not perform any transition (i.e.,
r(y)=\star
), even if the corresponding state
\corr(y)
may perform a transition (i.e.,
t(\corr(y))(a)\neq\star
for some
a
).

{exa}

As in Example
8
, consider the NPLTS on the left of Figure
1
.
The resolution
\mathcal{R}_{1}
in Figure
6
is a fully probabilistic resolution, while
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{3}
are not, since
x_{2}
is allowed to perform more than one transition, even if labelled by different actions.

In Figure
7
, we show three examples of fully probabilistic resolutions of the same NPLTS. Note that neither of them is a resolution. In
\mathcal{R}_{1}
, state
x_{1}
does not satisfy the first clause of the definition of resolution, since
x_{1}
does not move while its corresponding state in the original NPLTS does. In
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{3}
, state
x_{2}
respectively only performs a
b
-labelled transition and only performs a
c
-labelled transition. In a resolution, it should perform both.

x
{x_{1}}
a
x
\Delta_{3}
x_{1}
x_{2}

{x_{3}}

\frac{1}{2}
\frac{1}{2}

\frac{1}{4}

Figure 7.
Fully probabilistic resolutions (
\mathcal{R}_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}
,
\mathcal{R}_{3}
, from left to right)

As for resolutions, we can define
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}\colon Y\to[0,1]^{A^{*}}
for
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
a fully probabilistic resolution inductively for all
y\in Y
and all
w\in A^{*}
as

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad 1\text{;}

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\begin{cases}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w)&\text{ if }r(y)=\langle a,\Delta\rangle\text{,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

Given an NPLTS
(X,t)
, we define for
x\in X
and
w\in A^{*}
:

\displaystyle\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)=\bigsqcup\{\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)\,\mid\,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)\text{ is a fully probabilistic}

\displaystyle\text{ resolution of }(X,t)\text{ and }\corr(y)=x\}\text{.}

In
[
Cas18
]
(following
[
BDL14b
,
BDL14a
]
), two states
x
and
y
are defined to be
randomized
\sqcup
-trace equivalent
whenever
\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)=\lfloor\!\lfloor y\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)
, for all
w\in A^{*}
.
7
7

7

Actually,
[
Cas18
,
BDL14b
,
BDL14a
]
use a notion of resolution which is equal to our fully-probabilistic resolution modulo a tiny modification due to a mistake in
[
BDL14b
,
BDL14a
]
, as confirmed by the authors in a personal communication.
The following proposition guarantees that such equivalence coincides with
\equiv_{{B}}
.

Proposition 46
.

Let
(X,t)
be an NPLTS. For all
x\in X
and
w\in A^{*}
, it holds that

[\![x]\!]_{{B}}(w)=\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)=\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)
.

Proof 8.8
.

We first prove that
\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
be a resolution of
(X,t)
,
x\in X
, and
w\in A^{*}
. Let
y\in Y
such that
\corr(y)=x
. We show that there exists a fully probabilistic resolution
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of
(X,t)
with a state
z
such that
z
is mapped by the correspondence function of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
to
x
and such that
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(z)(w)
.

We define
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(Y\times A^{*},\corr^{\prime},r^{\prime})
as follows.
The correspondece function
\corr^{\prime}\colon Y\times A^{*}\to Y
is
\corr\mathrel{\circ}\pi_{1}
, namely
\corr^{\prime}(y,w^{\prime})=\corr(y)
for all
w^{\prime}\in A^{*}
. To define
r^{\prime}
, we use the notation
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{D}(Y\times A^{*})
to denote, for all
\Delta\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
and
w^{\prime}\in A^{*}
, the distribution over
Y\times A^{*}
given as

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta_{w^{\prime}}(y,w^{\prime\prime})&=&\begin{cases}\Delta(y)&\text{ if }w^{\prime}=w^{\prime\prime}\text{,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{array}

Now
r^{\prime}\colon Y\times A^{*}\to(A\times\mathcal{D}(Y\times A^{*}))+1
is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}r^{\prime}(y,\epsilon)&=&\star\\
r^{\prime}(y,aw^{\prime})&=&\begin{cases}\langle a,\Delta_{w^{\prime}}\rangle&\text{ if }r(y)(a)=\Delta\neq\star\\
\star&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{array}

First, it is necessary to observe that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is indeed a fully probabilistic system, that is, for every state there is at most one transition that can be performed.
Indeed, by taking as set of states of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
the set
Y\times A^{*}
, we guarantee that despite a state
y
in
\mathcal{R}
might perform different transitions reaching distributions over states (one for each label), only one of these transitions is actually performed by a corresponding state
(y,aw^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, namely the transition with label
a
. This allows us to move from a (reactive) resolution
\mathcal{R}
to a fully probabilistic resolution
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, while preserving the probability of performing traces. As we will prove below, for all
w
and
y
it holds
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,w)(w)
. To illustrate this with an example, suppose the resolution
\mathcal{R}
is the RPLTS with two states
y,y^{\prime}
and with
r(y)(a)=\delta_{y}
and
r(y)(b)=\delta_{y^{\prime}}
. Take the trace
ab
, for which we have
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(ab)=1
.
Then in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
we have
r^{\prime}(y,ab)=\langle a,\delta_{(y,b)}\rangle
and
r^{\prime}(y,b)=\langle b,\delta_{(y^{\prime},\epsilon)}\rangle
, which gives
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,ab)(ab)=1
.

We proceed by proving that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a fully probabilistic resolution of
(X,t)
.
Suppose that
r^{\prime}(y,w^{\prime})\neq\star
. Then
w^{\prime}=aw^{\prime\prime}
,
and
r^{\prime}(y,w^{\prime})=\langle a,\Delta_{w^{\prime\prime}}\rangle
with
\Delta=r(y)(a)
. Hence,
\mathcal{D}(\corr^{\prime})(\Delta_{w^{\prime\prime}})=\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta)
. Since
\mathcal{R}
is a resolution,
\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta)\in\convex(t(x)(a))
and therefore
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a fully probabilistic resolution.

We now prove that for all
w^{\prime}\in A^{*}

and for all
y\in Y
, it holds that
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w^{\prime})=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,w^{\prime})(w^{\prime})
.
The proof goes by induction on
w^{\prime}
.

If
w^{\prime}=\epsilon
then,
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(\epsilon)=1=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,\epsilon)(\epsilon)
.

If
w^{\prime}=aw^{\prime\prime}
and
r(y)(a)=\star
, then
r^{\prime}(y,w^{\prime})=\star
and
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,w^{\prime})(w^{\prime})=0=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w^{\prime})
.

If
w^{\prime}=aw^{\prime\prime}
and
r(y)(a)=\Delta\neq\star
, then
r^{\prime}(y,aw^{\prime\prime})=\langle a,\Delta_{w^{\prime\prime}}\rangle
and

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(IH)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R^{\prime}}}(y^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})(w^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(y^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})\in\supp(\Delta_{w^{\prime\prime}})}\Delta_{w^{\prime\prime}}(y^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R^{\prime}}}(y^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})(w^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R^{\prime}}}(y,aw^{\prime\prime})(aw^{\prime\prime})

Hence,
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,w)(w)
, with
\corr(y)=\corr^{\prime}(y,w)=x
.

We now prove that
\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor(w)\geq\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\corr,r)
be a fully probabilistic resolution of
(X,t)
,
x\in X
, and
w\in A^{*}
. Let
\corr(y)=x
. We show that there exists a resolution
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(Y^{\prime},\corr^{\prime},r^{\prime})
of
(X,t)
with a state
z
such that
\corr^{\prime}(z)=x
and
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)\leq\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(z)(w)
.
We define
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(Y^{\prime},\corr^{\prime},r^{\prime})
as follows:

•

Y^{\prime}=Y+X
is the (disjoint) union of
Y
and
X

•

\corr^{\prime}=\corr+\ide_{X}
that is for all
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime})&=&\begin{cases}\corr(y^{\prime})&\text{ if }y^{\prime}\in Y\text{;}\\
\ide_{X}(y^{\prime})&\text{ if }y^{\prime}\in X\text{.}\end{cases}\end{array}

•

r^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to(\mathcal{D}(Y^{\prime})+1)^{A}
is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}r^{\prime}(y^{\prime})(a)&=&\begin{cases}\star&\text{if $t(\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))(a)=\star$}\\
\Delta&\text{ if $y^{\prime}\in Y$ and }r(y^{\prime})=\langle a,\Delta\rangle\\
\Delta_{\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}),a}&\text{ otherwise}\\
\end{cases}\end{array}

where
\Delta_{x,a}
are defined like in the base case of the proof of Proposition
45
(namely, an arbitrary choice amongst the distributions in
t(x)(a)
).

We prove that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a resolution. For elements
y^{\prime}\in X
the conditions of Definition
8
are trivially satisfied (see the analogous construction used in the base case in the proof of Proposition
45
).
Suppose
y^{\prime}\in Y
.

(1)

By definition,
r^{\prime}(y^{\prime})(a)=\star
iff
t(\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))(a)=\star
.

(2)

If
r^{\prime}(y^{\prime})(a)\neq\star
, then we are either in the second or in the third case of the definition of
r^{\prime}
. If we are in the second case,
r^{\prime}(y^{\prime})(a)=\Delta
with
r(y^{\prime})=\langle a,\Delta\rangle
. Since
\Delta\in\mathcal{D}Y
we have
\mathcal{D}(\corr^{\prime})(\Delta)=\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta)
, and by the definition of fully probabilistic resolution, it holds that
\mathcal{D}(\corr)(\Delta)\in\convex(t(\corr(y^{\prime}))(a))
. Therefore
\mathcal{D}(\corr^{\prime})(\Delta)\in\convex(t(\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))(a))
.
If we are in the third case, we have
r^{\prime}(y^{\prime})(a)=\Delta_{\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}),a}
, with
\Delta_{\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}),a}\in t(\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))(a)
. By definition of
\corr^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{D}(\corr^{\prime})(\Delta_{\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}),a})=\Delta_{\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}),a}
. Therefore
\mathcal{D}(\corr^{\prime})(\Delta_{\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}),a})\in\convex(t(\corr^{\prime}(y^{\prime}))(a))
.

We conclude by showing that for all
y\in Y
and for all
w^{\prime}
,
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y,w^{\prime})\leq\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,w^{\prime})
. The proof goes by induction on
w^{\prime}
.
The case
w^{\prime}=\epsilon
is trivial, since
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y,\epsilon)=1=\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y,\epsilon)
.
For the inductive case, take
w^{\prime}=aw^{\prime\prime}
.
Suppose
r(y)=\langle a,\Delta\rangle
. Then, by definition of
r^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}(y)(a)=\Delta
, and

\displaystyle\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\supp(\Delta)}\Delta(y^{\prime})\cdot\pprob_{\mathcal{R^{\prime}}}(y^{\prime})(w^{\prime\prime})

(by IH)

\displaystyle=\pprob_{\mathcal{R^{\prime}}}(y)(aw^{\prime\prime})

Now suppose that
r(y)\neq\langle a,\Delta\rangle
. Then
r(y)=\star
and, by definition of
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}
,
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(aw^{\prime\prime})=0
, so there is nothing to prove.

Hence,
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}}(y)(w)\leq\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y)(w)
, with
\corr(y)=\corr^{\prime}(y)=x
.

Remark 47
.

The correspondence in Proposition
46
does not hold when infima are considered, instead of suprema. Indeed define
\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil_{fp}(w)
as expected, namely, by replacing
\bigsqcup
with
\bigsqcap
in
\lfloor\!\lfloor x\rfloor\!\rfloor_{fp}(w)
. Then for any state
x
of an arbitrary NPLTS it holds that
\lceil\!\lceil x\rceil\!\rceil_{fp}(w)=0
for all
w\neq\varepsilon
.
To see this, observe that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(\{y\},\corr^{\prime},r^{\prime})
with
\corr^{\prime}(y)=x
and
r^{\prime}(y)=\star
is always a fully probabilistic resolution, and that
\pprob_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(y)(w)=0
.

To avoid this problem, one typically modifies the definition of
\lceil\!\lceil\cdot\rceil\!\rceil_{fp}
by restricting only to those fully probabilistic resolutions that can perform a certain trace (see e.g.
[
BDL14b
,
BDL14a
]
). Instead, with our notion of resolution based on RPLTSs (Definition
8
), this problem does not arise and the definition of
\lceil\!\lceil\cdot\rceil\!\rceil
is totally analogous to the one of
\lfloor\!\lfloor\cdot\rfloor\!\rfloor
.

Why may, must, may-must? Trace equivalences as testing equivalences

The notion of resolution is at the basis not just of the definitions of trace equivalences for NPLTS investigated in the literature, but also of testing equivalences for nondeterministic and probabilistic processes
[
YL92
,
JHSY94
,
DGH+07
,
DGHM09
]
, extending the theory of testing equivalences for purely nondeterministic processes
[
DH84
]
(see Remark
29
).
In testing equivalences for nondeterministic and probabilistic processes,
we say that
x,y
are may testing equivalent if, for every test, they have the same greatest probabilities of passing the test, with respect to any resolution
\mathcal{R}
of the system resulting from the interaction between the test and the NPLTS. Analogously,
x,y
are must testing equivalent if the smallest probabilities coincide, and the may-must testing equivalence requires both the greatest and the smallest probabilities to coincide.

Now, take tests to be finite traces, and the probability of passing a given test in a resolution as the probability of performing the trace in the resolution. Then it becomes clear, by the correspondence between the local and the global view proven in Theorem
38
, that each of our three trace equivalences indeed coincides with the corresponding testing equivalence, when tests are finite traces.

9.
Conclusion and future work

We developed an algebra-and-coalgebra-based trace theory for systems with nondeterminism and probability, that covers intricate trace semantics from the literature. The abstract approach sheds light on all choices and leaves no space for ad-hoc solutions.

Although the combination of nondeterminism and probability has been posing challenges to abstract approaches, this new algebraic theory of traces for NPLTS shows that it can be dealt with in a smooth and uniform way.

In
[
Jac08
]
, Jacobs leaves open the question of whether the semantics that he obtains by exploiting the Kleisli traces
[
HJS07
]
for the monad
C
coincides with some scheduler based semantics. We would like to answer that such semantics corresponds to the may-must trace equivalence by our results and the correspondence between Kleisli traces and generalised determinisation studied in
[
JSS15
]
. However, we cannot directly derive this: for technical reasons (namely, the absence of a bottom element in the Kleisli category of
C
), Jacobs has to hack the general framework in
[
HJS07
]
and therefore the correspondence does not follow from known results. Providing an answer to this interesting question is left as future work.
